&&&&0&1&0&1&&&&&0&-1&0&-1&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&B&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&1&0&1&0&&&&&-1&0&-1&0&&&&&A&g\\
&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&0&1&0&1&&&&&0&-1&0&-1&&&&&B&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&1&0&1&0&&&&&-1&0&-1&0&A&h\\
&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&0&1&0&1&&&&&0&-1&0&-1&B&\\
\hline\cr 1&1&0&0&&&&&&&&&&&&&-1&-1&0&0&&&&&&&&&&&&&A&i\\
0&0&1&1&&&&&&&&&&&&&0&0&-1&-1&&&&&&&&&&&&&B&\\
\hline\cr&&&&1&1&0&0&&&&&&&&&&&&&-1&-1&0&0&&&&&&&&&A&j\\
&&&&0&0&1&1&&&&&&&&&&&&&0&0&-1&-1&&&&&&&&&B&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&1&1&0&0&&&&&&&&&&&&&-1&-1&0&0&&&&&A&k\\
&&&&&&&&0&0&1&1&&&&&&&&&&&&&0&0&-1&-1&&&&&B&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&&&&&1&1&0&0&&&&&&&&&&&&&-1&-1&0&0&A&\ell\\
&&&&&&&&&&&&0&0&1&1&&&&&&&&&&&&&0&0&-1&-1&B&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}}

\displaystyle d_{1}^{1,0}=\begin{array}[]{|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}}a&&b&&c&&d&&e&&f&&g&&h&&i&&j&&k&&\ell&&\\
A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&&\\
\hline\cr 1&1&&&-1&-1&&&&&&&&&&&-1&-1&1&1&&&&&\alpha\\
\hline\cr&&1&1&&&-1&-1&&&&&&&&&&&&&-1&-1&1&1&\beta\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&1&1&&&-1&-1&&&-1&-1&&&1&1&&&\gamma\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&&&1&1&&&-1&-1&&&-1&-1&&&1&1&\delta\\
\hline\cr&&&&1&1&-1&-1&&&&&-1&-1&1&1&&&&&&&&&\varepsilon\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

\displaystyle d_{1}^{2,0}=\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|c}\alpha&\beta&\gamma&\delta&\varepsilon&\\
\hline\cr 0&0&0&0&0&vol\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Here,
\{A,B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}\}
and
\{A,B\}
represent irreps of
D_{2}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
groups, respectively.
It is straightforward to see that
d_{1}^{1,0}d_{1}^{0,0}=d_{1}^{2,0}d_{1}^{1,0}=0
.
The
E_{2}
-page is found as

\displaystyle E_{2}^{0,0}={\rm Ker\,}(d_{1}^{0,0})=\mathbb{Z}^{13},

(94)

\displaystyle E_{2}^{1,0}={\rm Ker\,}(d_{1}^{1,0})/{\rm Im\,}(d_{1}^{0,0})=\mathbb{Z}_{2},

(95)

\displaystyle E_{2}^{2,0}={\rm Ker\,}(d_{1}^{2,0})/{\rm Im\,}(d_{1}^{1,0})=0,

(96)

\displaystyle E_{2}^{3,0}={\rm Coker\,}(d_{1}^{2,0})=\mathbb{Z}.

(97)

Since
E_{3}^{3,0}=E_{2}^{3,0}=\mathbb{Z}
is nonzero, the third differential
d_{3}^{0,2}:E_{3}^{0,2}\to E_{3}^{3,0}
can be nontrivial.
One can find that
d_{3}^{0,2}
is trivial:
We note that
E_{2}^{3,0}=E_{1}^{3,0}
arises from the 3-cell
vol
which has no symmetry left, which implies
E_{2}^{3,0}=\mathbb{Z}
is the trivial irrep
A
under
D_{2}
.
On the other hand, as pointed out in Sec.
III.1
, the third differential
d_{3}^{0,0}
should accompany a band inversion between a pair of different irreps, which means the inverse image
(d_{3}^{0,0})^{-1}
should be a nontrivial irrep under
D_{2}
, i.e.
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
or
B_{3}
.
However, we do not have a homomorphism from irreps
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
, and
B_{3}
to the trivial irrep
A
.
Therefore,
d_{3}^{0,0}
is the zero map, and
E_{2}
-page is the limit
E_{\infty}=E_{2}
.

It should be noticed that
E_{2}^{1,0}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
means the appearance of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant
(-1)^{\nu}\in\{\pm 1\}
defined on the 1-skeleton
X_{1}
in class AIII, of which the construction is described in Sec.
III.3.1
.
We find that the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant
(-1)^{\nu}
is the parity of the half of the 3d winding number:
The
K
-group of degree -1 fits into the exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\underbrace{\mathbb{Z}}_{E_{2}^{3,0},w_{3d}=4}\to K^{-1}_{D_{2}}(T^{3})\to\underbrace{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{E_{2}^{1,0},\nu\equiv 1}\to 0.

(98)

Here,
E_{2}^{3,0}=\mathbb{Z}
is generated by a Hamiltonian with the 3d winding number
w_{3d}
of
4
since the fundamental region of BZ is
\frac{1}{4}BZ
and each quarter contributes by 1 to the 3d winding number.
On the one hand, the
K
-group
K^{-1}_{D_{2}}(T^{3})
is computed by the Clifford algebra
Morimoto and Furusaki 2013
;
Shiozaki and Sato 2014
, and we find that
K^{-1}_{D_{2}}(T^{3})=\mathbb{Z}
, and
K^{-1}_{D_{2}}(T^{3})
is generated by a Hamiltonian with the 3d winding number
w_{3d}=2
.
5
5

5

Let
H=k_{x}\gamma_{x}+k_{y}\gamma_{y}+k_{z}\gamma_{z}+M
be the Dirac Hamiltonian,
\Gamma
a chiral operator, and
U_{x}
and
U_{y}
the two-fold rotation operators along
x
and
y
-axes, respectively.
The classification of symmetry-respecting mass
M
is equivalent to the classification of the extension of the complex Clifford algebra
\{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{y},\gamma_{z},\Gamma\}\otimes Cl_{2}\to\{\gamma_{x},\gamma_{y},\gamma_{z},\Gamma,M\}\otimes Cl_{2}
, where
Cl_{2}
is the complex Clifford algebra generated by
\{U_{x}\gamma_{y}\gamma_{z},U_{y}\gamma_{x}\gamma_{z}\}
.
The classification is recast as that for
3d
class AIII without symmetry, i.e.
\mathbb{Z}
.
Also, due to the commuting algebra
Cl_{2}
, the
3d
winding number
w_{3d}
should be an even integer.

This implies that the extension (
98
) is nontrivial.
Otherwise, the
K
group
K^{-1}_{D_{2}}(T^{3})
becomes
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Therefore, there is the relation

\displaystyle(-1)^{\nu}=(-1)^{w_{3d}/2}.

(99)

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant
\nu
is an analog of the symmetry indicator:
If a band structure in class AIII is fully gapped and the 3d winding number is zero, then
\nu\equiv 0
.
Therefore, if
\nu\equiv 1
then the system is deformable to a gapped band structure with a finite 3d winding number
w_{3d}\equiv 2\ ({\rm mod\ }4)
without closing a gap on the 1-skeleton.

Construction of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant

Let
q({\bm{k}})
be the off-diagonal part of the Hamiltonian
H({\bm{k}})=\begin{pmatrix}0&q({\bm{k}})\\
q({\bm{k}})^{{\dagger}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}
in the basis so that the chiral operator is
\Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
.
WLOG,
q({\bm{k}})
is assumed to be a unitary matrix.
The
D_{2}
symmetry matrices in spinless systems are written as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}U_{x}({\bm{k}})q({\bm{k}})=q(C_{2x}{\bm{k}})U_{x}({\bm{k}}),\\
U_{x}(C_{2x}{\bm{k}})U_{x}({\bm{k}})=1,\\
U_{y}({\bm{k}})q({\bm{k}})=q(C_{2y}{\bm{k}})U_{y}({\bm{k}}),\\
U_{y}(C_{2y}{\bm{k}})U_{y}({\bm{k}})=1,\\
U_{x}(C_{2y}{\bm{k}})U_{y}({\bm{k}})=U_{y}(C_{2x}{\bm{k}})U_{x}({\bm{k}}).\end{array}\right.

On the symmetric lines (1-cells), the matrix
q({\bm{k}})
becomes a block-diagonal form as

\displaystyle q({\bm{k}})=\begin{pmatrix}q_{A}({\bm{k}})&\\
&q_{B}({\bm{k}})\\
\end{pmatrix},\qquad({\bm{k}}\in\mbox{1-cells}),

(105)

according to the symmetry of the little group
G_{{\bm{k}}}
.
In the same way, at the high-symmetry points (0-cells), the matrix
q({\bm{k}})
is decomposed as

\displaystyle q({\bm{k}})=\begin{pmatrix}q_{A}({\bm{k}})&&&\\
&q_{B_{1}}({\bm{k}})&&\\
&&q_{B_{2}}({\bm{k}})&\\
&&&q_{B_{3}}({\bm{k}})\\
\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle({\bm{k}}\in\mbox{0-cells}).

(106)

Let us focus on the determinant of the matrix
q({\bm{k}})
within the subsectors.
We denote
e^{i\theta_{A/B}({\bm{k}})}=\det q_{A/B}({\bm{k}})
for
{\bm{k}}\in
1-cells and
e^{i\phi_{A/B_{i}}({\bm{k}})}=\det q_{A/B_{i}}({\bm{k}})

(i=1,2,3)
for
{\bm{k}}\in
0-cells.
The origin of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant is the fact that the
U(1)
phases
e^{i\theta_{A/B}({\bm{k}})}
on 1-cells do not fully determine the
U(1)
phases
e^{i\phi_{A/B_{i}}({\bm{k}})}
at 0-cells:
For instance, around the
\Gamma
point, the compatibility relation reads

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}e^{i\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in a)}|_{{\bm{k}}\to\Gamma}=e^{i(\phi_{B_{1}}(\Gamma)+\phi_{B_{2}}(\Gamma))},\\
e^{i\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in e)}|_{{\bm{k}}\to\Gamma}=e^{i(\phi_{B_{1}}(\Gamma)+\phi_{B_{3}}(\Gamma))},\\
e^{i\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in i)}|_{{\bm{k}}\to\Gamma}=e^{i(\phi_{B_{2}}(\Gamma)+\phi_{B_{3}}(\Gamma))},\end{array}\right.

from which we have

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}e^{2i\phi_{B_{1}}(\Gamma)}=e^{i\{\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in a)+\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in e)-\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in i)\}}|_{{\bm{k}}\to\Gamma},\\
e^{2i\phi_{B_{2}}(\Gamma)}=e^{i\{\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in a)-\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in e)+\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in i)\}}|_{{\bm{k}}\to\Gamma},\\
e^{2i\phi_{B_{3}}(\Gamma)}=e^{i\{-\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in a)+\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in e)+\theta_{B}({\bm{k}}\in i)\}}|_{{\bm{k}}\to\Gamma},\end{array}\right.

where
a,e,i
indicate
1
-cells shown in Fig.
15
.
Thus,
U(1)
phases
\phi_{B_{i}}(\Gamma)
are fixed by the
U(1)
phases on 1-cells up to a
\pi
-phase.
Similar relations hold true for other
0
-cells.
From these
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
ambiguities, one can define a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant

\displaystyle(-1)^{\nu}

\displaystyle:=\exp\Big[\frac{i}{2}\int_{a-b-c+d-e+f+g-h-i+j+k-\ell}d\theta_{B}({\bm{k}})

\displaystyle+i\sum_{{\bm{k}}\in\Gamma,S,U,T}\phi_{B_{3}}({\bm{k}})-i\sum_{{\bm{k}}\in X,Y,Z,R}\phi_{B_{3}}({\bm{k}})\Big].

(113)

The constraint (
III.3.1
) (and same ones for other 0-cells) leads to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
quantization
\{(-1)^{\nu}\}^{2}=1
.
We have checked that a model Hamiltonian with 3d winding number
w_{3d}=2
gives
(-1)^{\nu}=-1
.
6
6

6

From the Clifford algebra, a model Hamiltonian is given by

H=\sin k_{x}\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}\mu_{0}+\sin k_{y}\sigma_{y}\tau_{x}\mu_{0}+\sin k_{z}\sigma_{z}\tau_{x}\mu_{0}+(m+\cos k_{x}+\cos k_{y}+\cos k_{z})\sigma_{0}\tau_{y}\mu_{0}
,

\Gamma=\sigma_{0}\tau_{z}\mu_{0}
,
U_{x}=\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}\mu_{x},U_{y}=\sigma_{y}\tau_{0}\mu_{y}
, where
\sigma_{i},\tau_{i},\mu_{i}(i=0,x,y,z)
are Pauli matrices.

III.3.2
F222

Figure 16:
A
D_{2}
-equivariant cell decomposition of BZ for the space group
F222
. The figure shows the a quarter of BZ.

The second example is the space group
F222
(No.
22
, symmorphic).
The Bravais lattice is face-centered and the reciprocal lattice vectors (in the unit of
2\pi
) are
\bm{b}_{1}=(-1,1,1),\bm{b}_{2}=(1,-1,1)
and
\bm{b}_{3}=(1,1,-1)
.
The point group is
D_{2}
again and is generated by
C_{2x}
and
C_{2y}
.
A
D_{2}
-equivariant cell decomposition is shown in Fig.
16
.
The list of
p
-cells (
p=0,1,2,3
) is as follows.

\displaystyle\mbox{0-cells}=\{\Gamma=(0,0,0),X=(1,0,0),

\displaystyle\qquad Y=(0,1,0),M=(1,1,0)\},

\displaystyle\mbox{1-cells}=\{a,b,c,d,e,f\},

\displaystyle\mbox{2-cells}=\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\},

\displaystyle\mbox{3-cells}=\{vol\ (\mbox{$\frac{1}{4}$BZ shown in Fig.~\ref{fig:22}})\}.

The 2-cells
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are equivalent to
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, respectively:

\alpha^{\prime}=C_{2y}\alpha+\bm{b}_{1}+\bm{b}_{2}+\bm{b}_{3}
,

\beta^{\prime}=C_{2x}\beta+\bm{b}_{1}
and

\gamma^{\prime}=C_{2z}\gamma+\bm{b}_{3}
.
The similar equivalence relations hold true for 1- and 0-cells.
An interesting feature is found in
E_{2}^{2,0}
, the
2d
class A topological invariant.
For both factor systems of spinful and spinless electrons, the first differentials
d_{1}^{1,0}
and
d_{1}^{2,0}
are given by

\displaystyle d_{1}^{1,0}=\begin{array}[]{|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}c@{}|c@{}}a&&b&&c&&d&&e&&f&\\
A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&A&B&\\
\hline\cr 1&1&&&1&1&-1&-1&&&1&1&\alpha\\
\hline\cr&&1&1&1&1&&&-1&-1&-1&-1&\beta\\
\hline\cr 1&1&1&1&&&1&1&1&1&&&\gamma\\
\hline\cr\end{array},

\displaystyle d_{1}^{2,0}=\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c}\alpha&\beta&\gamma&\\
\hline\cr 0&0&0&vol\\
\hline\cr\end{array},

where
A
(
B
) represents the trivial (nontrivial) irrep of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
It is found that
E_{2}^{2,0}={\rm Ker\,}(d_{1}^{2,0})/{\rm Im\,}(d_{1}^{1,0})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which means that the existence of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-valued class A topological invariant defined on the 2-skeleton
X_{2}
.

The appearance of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant is understood from that the 2-dimensional boundary
\partial(\frac{1}{4}BZ)
of the quarter of BZ has the same structure as the real projective plane
RP^{2}
owing to the identification of the 2-cells.
Similar to the formula for the torsion part of the first Chern class
c_{1}\in H^{2}(RP^{2},\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}

Freed 1986
;
Shiozaki et al. 2017
, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant for
F222
is defined by

\displaystyle(-1)^{\nu}:=\exp\left[\int_{a+b+c}{\rm tr\,}{\cal A}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\alpha+\beta+\gamma}{\rm tr\,}{\cal F}\right],

(121)

with
{\cal A}
and
{\cal F}
the Berry connection and curvature, respectively.
Notice that the path
a+b+c
is closed since
\Gamma^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\Gamma
.
The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-quantization follows from the Stokes’ theorem applied to the boundary
\partial(\alpha+\beta+\gamma)=a+b+c+a^{\prime}+b^{\prime}+c^{\prime}
.
We have checked that an atomic insulator generates a
K
-group with
(-1)^{\nu}=-1
.
7
7

7

See
Shiozaki et al.
for the detail. K.S. thanks Judith Höller for pointing out this fact.

This example implies that topological invariants defined on a two-dimensional surface in the momentum space do not necessarily yield a nontrivial topological insulator with a surface state.

III.3.3
P3_{1}
and torsion invariant

This section gives an example of the AHSS for a nonsymmorphic space group and shows that the
n
-fold screw axis leads to a
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
torsion invariant in class AIII.

Figure 17:
A
C_{3}
-equivariant cell decomposition of BZ.
The figure shows one third of the BZ.

Let us consider the space group
P3_{1}
(No.144) in spinless systems.
The Bravais lattice is primitive and the point group is
C_{3}
, and the 3-fold rotation
C_{3z}
is accompanied by the nonprimitive lattice translation
\frac{1}{3}\hat{z}
.
The
C_{3}
group acts on the
k
-space with the twist
U(C_{3z}^{2}{\bm{k}})U(C_{3z}{\bm{k}})U({\bm{k}})=e^{-ik_{z}}
.
At the screw axes, the three irreps are labeled by eigenvalues
\lambda\in\{e^{-ik_{z}/3},\omega e^{-ik_{z}/3},\omega^{2}e^{-ik_{z}/3}\}
(
\omega=e^{-2\pi i/3}
) which are cyclically permuted by the shift
k_{z}\mapsto k_{z}+2\pi
.

Figure 18:
The structure of Bloch states on a
3
-fold screw axis.

A
C_{3}
-equivariant cell decomposition is given as in Fig.
17
.
The list of
p
-cells (
p=0,1,2,3
) is as follows.

\displaystyle\mbox{0-cells}=\{\Gamma,K,K^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle\mbox{1-cells}=\{a,b,c,d,e\},

\displaystyle\mbox{2-cells}=\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma\},

\displaystyle\mbox{3-cells}=\{vol\ (\mbox{$\frac{1}{3}$BZ shown in Fig.~\ref{fig:144}})\}.

We should take care of the compatibility relation on the screw axes.
See Fig.
18
.
An irrep on the 1-cell
a
is connected to the different irreps at
k_{z}=0
and
k_{z}=2\pi
.
The block matrix from
\Gamma
to
a
of the differential
d_{1}^{0,0}
is given by

\displaystyle d_{1}^{0,0}|_{\Gamma\to a}=\begin{array}[]{|ccc|cc}\Gamma&&&&\\
1&\omega&\omega^{2}&&\\
\hline\cr 1&-1&0&e^{-ik_{z}/3}&a\\
0&1&-1&\omega e^{-ik_{z}/3}&\\
-1&0&1&\omega^{2}e^{-ik_{z}/3}&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Taking all contributions into account, we obtain

\displaystyle E_{2}^{0,0}=\mathbb{Z},\quad E_{2}^{1,0}=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{3}^{2},

\displaystyle E_{2}^{2,0}=\mathbb{Z},\quad E_{2}^{3,0}=\mathbb{Z}.

(127)

The torsion in
E_{2}^{1,0}
means that there are two
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
invariants defined on the 1-skeleton
X_{1}
.
On the one hand, the class AIII
K
-group is found to be

K^{\tau+1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}(T^{3})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{3}
from the Mayer-Vietoris sequence,
8
8

8

Proof.
Applying the Mayer-Vietoris sequence to the
k_{z}
-direction, we have

\displaystyle 0\to K^{\tau+0}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}(T^{3})\to K\oplus K\xrightarrow{\Delta}K\oplus K\to K^{\tau+1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}(T^{3})\to 0,

where
K=K^{0}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}(T^{2})\cong R(\mathbb{Z}_{3})\oplus R(\mathbb{Z}_{3})\oplus(1-t)
is the class A
K
-group with the
C_{3}
rotation symmetry.
Shiozaki et al. 2017

Here,
R(\mathbb{Z}_{3})=\mathbb{Z}[t]/(1-t^{3})
is the representation ring of
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
and
(1-t)
is an
R(\mathbb{Z}_{3})
-ideal.
The homomorphism
\Delta
is given by
(x,y)\mapsto(x-y,x-ty)
.
Then, we have
K^{\tau+0}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}(T^{3})\cong{\rm Ker\,}\Delta\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

K^{\tau+1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{3}}(T^{3})\cong{\rm Coker\,}\Delta\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{3}
.

which implies that one of the two
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
invariants is determined by the 3d winding number
w_{3d}
.

The construction of the screw
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
invariant

In general, a pair of
n
-fold screw axes gives rise to a
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
invariant in class AIII, which is the generalization of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant in
2d
class AIII with the glide symmetry.
Shiozaki et al. 2017

Let
q(k_{z})
be the off-diagonal part of the Hamiltonian
H(k_{z})=\begin{pmatrix}0&q(k_{z})\\
q(k_{z})^{{\dagger}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}
on a
n
-fold screw axis.
The matrix
q(k_{z})
splits into subsectors as

\displaystyle q(k_{z})=q_{0}(k_{z})\oplus q_{1}(k_{z})\oplus\cdots\oplus q_{n-1}(k_{z})

(128)

with respect to the eigenvalues
\omega^{j}e^{-ik_{z}/n}(j=0,1,\dots,n-1)
with
\omega=e^{-2\pi i/n}
.
Because of the twist,
q_{j}(k_{z})
are cyclically permuted as
q_{j}(k_{z}+2\pi)=q_{j+1}(k_{z})
.
We introduce the following
U(1)
-valued quantity

\displaystyle e^{i\phi(k_{z})}:=\det q_{0}(k_{z})\cdot\exp\left[\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}\frac{n-j-1}{n}\int_{k_{z}}^{k_{z}+2\pi}dk_{z}^{\prime}\partial_{k_{z}^{\prime}}\log\det q_{j}(k_{z}^{\prime})\right]

(129)

so that its
n
th power becomes the total determinant
e^{in\phi(k_{z})}=\det q(k_{z})
.
Suppose that there are two
n
-fold screw axes at
(k_{x},k_{y})=X
and
Y
.
The
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
invariant
e^{2\pi i\nu/n}
is defined to be

\displaystyle e^{2\pi i\nu/n}:=\exp\left[i\phi(X,k_{z})-i\phi(Y,k_{z})-\frac{1}{n}\int_{X\to Y}d{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}\log\det q({\bm{k}},k_{z})\right].

(130)

It is easy to show that
\{e^{2\pi i\nu/n}\}^{n}=1
, i.e.
e^{2\pi i\nu/n}
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
quantized
U(1)
phases.

For the space group
P3_{1}
, a nontrivial model showing
\nu=1
is given by putting a single SSH chain along the
x
-direction and extending to the
3d
lattice by group elements of the space group
P3_{1}
.

III.3.4
P\bar{1}

The final example is the space group
P\bar{1}
(No.2).
We illustrate how the representation enforced Weyl semimetal
Turner et al. 2012
appears in the AHSS.

Figure 19:
A
C_{i}
-equivariant cell decomposition of the BZ. The figure shows a half of the BZ.

The Bravais lattice is primitive and the point group is
C_{i}(\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2})
generated by the inversion
I
.
Fig.
19
shows a
C_{i}
-equivariant cell decomposition, where the
p
-cells are given as

\displaystyle\mbox{0-cells}=\{\Gamma,X,Y,S,Z,U,T,R\},

\displaystyle\mbox{1-cells}=\{a,b,c,d,e,f,g\},

\displaystyle\mbox{2-cells}=\{\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\},

\displaystyle\mbox{3-cells}=\{vol\ (\mbox{$\frac{1}{2}$BZ shown in Fig.~\ref{fig:2}})\}.

An important point is that the orientation of the
2
-cell
\alpha(\beta)
is the same as the equivalent 2-cell
\alpha^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime})
thereof since the inversion
I
acts on the
k_{z}=0(\pi)
plane as the
2
-fold rotation
C_{2z}
.
Then, the first differential
d_{1}^{2,0}:\mathbb{Z}^{4}\to\mathbb{Z}
is

\displaystyle d_{1}^{2,0}=\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c}\alpha&\beta&\gamma&\delta&\\
\hline\cr 2&-2&0&0&vol\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Apparently, we have
E_{2}^{3,0}=E_{1}^{3,0}/{\rm Im\,}(d_{1}^{2,0})=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
This means that odd numbers of Weyl points inside
\frac{1}{2}
BZ region can not be trivialized from the pair creation of Weyl points from 2-cells.

From a straightforward calculation, we find that
E_{2}^{0,0}=\mathbb{Z}^{9}
.
The third differential
d_{3}^{0,2}:\mathbb{Z}^{9}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
can be nontrivial, and one can show this is the case.
The explicit form of
d_{3}^{0,2}
is given by
Turner et al. 2012

\displaystyle d_{3}^{0,2}:\mathbb{Z}^{9}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2},

\displaystyle(n,\{n_{-}^{{\bm{k}}}\}_{{\bm{k}}\in{\rm 0-cells}})\mapsto(-1)^{\nu}:=(-1)^{\sum_{{\bm{k}}\in{\rm 0-cells}}n^{{\bm{k}}}_{-}},

(133)

where
n
is the filling number and
n^{{\bm{k}}}_{-}
is the number of irreps with
I=-1
.
9
9

9

A quick derivation of (
133
) is as follows.
On the
k_{z}=0
and
k_{z}=\pi
planes, the parity of the Chern number is constrained as
(-1)^{ch|_{k_{z}=0}}=(-1)^{\sum_{{\bm{k}}\in\Gamma,X,Y,S}n^{{\bm{k}}}_{-}}
and
(-1)^{ch|_{k_{z}=\pi}}=(-1)^{\sum_{{\bm{k}}\in Z,U,R,T}n^{{\bm{k}}}_{-}}
.
If the band structure is fully gapped, the Chern number should be uniform
ch|_{k_{z}=0}=ch|_{k_{z}=\pi}
, which implies
(-1)^{\nu}=1
.

Alternatively, as we seen in Sec.
III
, the third differential represents the band inversion resulting in the creation of Weyl points from
0
-cells.
Around the
\Gamma
point, a model Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle H(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})=(k^{2}-\mu)\sigma_{z}+k_{x}\sigma_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{y},

\displaystyle I=\sigma_{z}.

(134)

It is clear that when
\mu
passes zero, the band inversion between
I=1
and
I=-1
occurs, and a pair of Weyl points is pumped on the
k_{z}
axis.

IV
The AHSS with antiunitary symmetry

In this section, we formulate the AHSS for band theory in the presence of TRS and/or PHS.
First, we give the mathematical detail of the AHSS in Sec.
IV.1
.
In Sec.
IV.2
, as a warm-up, we calculate the AHSS for 2-dimensional systems without space group symmetry and reproduce the classification table for 2-dimensions.
Schnyder et al. 2008

In Sec.
IV.3
, we describe how to determine the
E_{1}
-page in general.
Although one can readily determine the first differential
d_{1}
using the compatibility relation, it is not straightforward to give the higher-differentials
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
.
We sketch the Hamiltonian formalism for
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
in Sec.
IV.4
.
We present some examples of the AHSS in Sec.
IV.6
.
Some technical details relevant to this section are in Appendices
A
and
C
.

IV.1
Formulation of the AHSS for general symmetry classes

In this section, we formulate the AHSS for general symmetry classes including antiunitary symmetry.
Since, the relationship among the sequences of
E_{r}
-pages and
r
-th differentials
d_{r}
is almost the same as Sec.
III.1
, we briefly sketch the mathematical formulation.

Let
G
be the symmetry group and
X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset X_{2}\subset X_{3}=T^{3}
be a
G
-filtration of the BZ torus associated to a cell decomposition.
Let
(\phi,c,\tau)
be the data of a symmetry class, where
\phi,c:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
indicate whether
g\in G
is unitary or antiunitary and symmetry or antisymmetry,
10
10

10

g\in G
is said antisymmetry if
g
anticommutes with the Hamiltonian like the particle-hole symmetry and the chiral symmetry.
respectively, and
\tau=\tau_{g,h}({\bm{k}})(g,h\in G,{\bm{k}}\in T^{3})
is the factor system for a given magnetic space group.

\tau_{g,h}({\bm{k}})
also depends on representations of the superconducting gap function of the point group.
Let
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})
be the twisted equivariant
K
-group of the BZ torus
T^{3}
,
Freed and Moore 2013
;
Shiozaki et al. 2017
;
Gomi
where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is the integer grading defined by adding chiral symmetries (see Sec. IV in Ref.
Shiozaki et al. 2017
).
The Bott periodicity
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})\cong{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n+8}_{G}(T^{3})
holds true.
In the absence of antiunitary symmetry (meaning the case where
\phi:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is trivial), the period of the Bott periodicity reduces to two,
K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})\cong K^{(\tau,c)-n+2}_{G}(T^{3})
.

The
E_{1}
-page of the AHSS is defined to be the
K
-group of the pair
(X_{p},X_{p-1})
,

\displaystyle E_{1}^{p,-n}:={}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-(n-p)}_{G}(X_{p},X_{p-1}).

(135)

Here, the
K
-group over the pair
(X_{p},X_{p-1})
means the classification of gaped Hamiltonians over the
p
-skeleton
X_{p}
which are constant on the
(p-1)
-skeleton
X_{p-1}
.
From the definition of
p
-skeletons, this
K
-group is recast as the direct sum of
K
-groups of orbits of
p
-cells,

\displaystyle E_{1}^{p,-n}

\displaystyle\cong{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-(n-p)}_{G}(\coprod_{j\in I^{p}_{\rm orb}}G/G_{D^{p}_{j}}\times D^{p}_{j},\coprod_{j\in I^{p}_{\rm orb}}G/G_{D^{p}_{j}}\times\partial D^{p}_{j})

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{j\in I^{p}_{\rm orb}}{}^{\phi|_{D^{p}_{j}}}K^{(\tau,c)|_{D^{p}_{j}}-(n-p)}_{G_{D^{p}_{j}}}(D^{p}_{j},\partial D^{p}_{j}).

(136)

Here,
D^{p}_{j}
is a representative
p
-cell for the orbit
j
, and
(\phi,c,\tau)|_{D^{p}_{j}}=(\phi|_{D^{p}_{j}},c|_{D^{p}_{j}}.\tau|_{D^{p}_{j}})
means the data
(\phi,c,\tau)
of the symmetry class restricted to a
p
-cell
D^{p}_{j}
, i.e. the data
(\phi,c,\tau)
for the little group
G_{D^{p}_{j}}
.
We find that the following four interpretations of the group
E_{1}^{p,-n}
.

(I)—

E_{1}^{p,-n}
is the direct sum of the
K
-groups over orbits of
p
-cells for topological insulators
H_{\rm TI}({\bm{k}})
with the symmetry class of the integer grading
(n-p)
.
On each
p
-cell
D^{p}_{j}
, a gapped Hamiltonian obeys the boundary condition so that it is constant on the boundary
\partial D^{p}_{j}
, which implies that
H_{\rm TI}({\bm{k}})
should be a massive Dirac Hamiltonian
H_{\rm TI}({\bm{k}})\sim\sum_{\mu=1}^{p}k_{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}+(m-\epsilon k^{2})\gamma_{p+1}
.

By the use of the bulk-boundary correspondence,
Shiozaki et al. 2017

E_{1}^{p,-n}
is also identified with the group classifying topological gapless states with a shift of integer grading:

(II)—

E_{1}^{p,-n}
is the direct sum of the
K
-groups over orbits of
p
-cells for topological gapless states
H_{\rm TGS}({\bm{k}})
with the symmetry class of integer grading
(n-p+1)
.
On each
p
-cell
D^{p}_{j}
, the spectrum is gapped on the boundary, which implies that
H_{\rm TGS}({\bm{k}})
should be a massless Dirac Hamiltonian
H_{\rm TGS}({\bm{k}})\sim\sum_{\mu=1}^{p}k_{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}
.

In the same way as Sec.
III.2.1
, for
p\geq 1
, applying the bulk-boundary correspondence to the
(p-1)
-dimensional sphere
S^{p-1}
surrounding the Dirac point of the topological gapless state in the
p
-cell, we find that the
E_{1}
-page is viewed as the group classifying topological singular points in
p
-cells:

(III)—

E_{1}^{p,-n}
is the direct sum of the
K
-groups over orbits of
p
-cells for topological singular points
H_{\rm TSP}({\bm{k}})
with the symmetry class of integer grading
(n-p+2)
.

The
E_{1}
-page is further deformed as follows.
By shrinking the boundary on each
p
-cell, the pair
(D^{p}_{j},\partial D^{p}_{j})
is considered as the
p
-dimensional sphere
D^{p}_{j}/\partial D^{p}_{j}\cong S^{p}_{j}
.
Using the Thom isomorphism, we have

\displaystyle E_{1}^{p,-n}

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{j\in I^{p}_{\rm orb}}{}^{\phi|_{D^{p}_{j}}}\widetilde{K}^{(\tau,c)|_{D^{p}_{j}}-(n-p)}_{G_{D^{p}_{j}}}(D^{p}_{j}/\partial D^{p}_{j})

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{j\in I^{p}_{\rm orb}}{}^{\phi|_{D^{p}_{j}}}K^{(\tau,c)|_{D^{p}_{j}}-n}_{G_{D^{p}_{j}}}(D^{p}_{j}),

(137)

where
{}^{\phi|_{D^{p}_{j}}}\widetilde{K}^{(\tau,c)|_{D^{p}_{j}}-(n-p)}_{G_{D^{p}_{j}}}(D^{p}_{j}/\partial D^{p}_{j})
meant the reduced
K
-theory.

{}^{\phi|_{D^{p}_{j}}}K^{(\tau,c)|_{D^{p}_{j}}-n}_{G_{D^{p}_{j}}}(D^{p}_{j})
is the
K
-group over the
p
-cell
D^{p}_{j}
.
Therefore, we arrived at the following formula to give the
E_{1}
-page:

(IV)—

E_{1}^{p,-n}
is the direct sum of the
K
-groups for the representations on
p
-cells.
Each
K
-group
{}^{\phi|_{D^{p}_{j}}}K^{(\tau,c)|_{D^{p}_{j}}-n}_{G_{D^{p}_{j}}}(D^{p}_{j})
represents the space of representations on the
p
-cell
D^{p}_{j}
with the symmetry class of the integer grading
n
.

This enables us to compute the
E_{1}
-page quickly by looking at what the symmetry class realized at the
p
-cell
D^{p}_{j}
is (See Sec.
IV.3
).

We define the first differential

\displaystyle d_{1}^{p,-n}:E_{1}^{p,-n}\to E_{1}^{p+1,-n}

(138)

in the same way as in Sec.
III.1
.
The homomorphism
d_{1}^{p,-n}
describes how irreps at
p
-cells are mapped to representations at adjacent
(p+1)
-cells.
From the meaning (II) of the
E_{1}
-page,
d_{1}^{p,-n}
can be also viewed as the creation of stable gapless points in
(p+1)
-cells from
p
-cells.
It holds that
d_{1}\circ d_{1}=0
.
The
E_{2}
-page is defined by the cohomology of
d_{1}
,

\displaystyle E_{2}^{p,-n}:={\rm Ker\,}(d_{1}^{p,-n})/{\rm Im\,}(d_{1}^{p-1,-n}).

(139)

We have the second differential in the
E_{2}
-page

\displaystyle d_{2}^{p,-n}:E_{2}^{p,-n}\to E_{2}^{p+2,-(n+1)}.

(140)

This expresses the creations of topological gapless points from a
p
-cell to adjacent
(p+2)
-cells, as shown in Fig.
20
.

Figure 20:

The second differential
d_{2}^{p,-n}
can be viewed as the creation of gapless points from
p
-cells to adjacent
(p+2)
-cells.

Similarly,
d_{2}\circ d_{2}=0
holds true and the
E_{3}
-page is defined to be

\displaystyle E_{3}^{p,-n}:={\rm Ker\,}(d_{2}^{p,-n})/{\rm Im\,}(d_{2}^{p-2,-(n-1)}),

(141)

and we define the third differential

\displaystyle d_{3}^{p,-n}:E_{3}^{p,-n}\to E_{3}^{p+3,-(n+2)}

(142)

which represents the creation of topological gapless points from 0-cells to adjacent
3
-cells.
We also have the
E_{4}
-page

\displaystyle E_{4}^{p,-n}:={\rm Ker\,}(d_{3}^{p,-n})/{\rm Im\,}(d_{3}^{p-3,-(n-2)}).

(143)

From the dimensional reason, the fourth differential
d_{4}^{p,-n}:E_{4}^{p,-n}\to E_{4}^{p+4,-(n+3)}
is trivial for 3-space dimensions.
This means that the
E_{4}
page is the limit
E_{\infty}=E_{4}
.

The limiting page
E_{\infty}=E_{\infty}^{p,-n}
approximates the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})
.
The topological invariants characterizing the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})
are given by “sticking” the local contributions
E_{\infty}^{p,-(n+p)}
arising from
p
-cells together appropriately. This is an extension problem in the algebraic point of view.
The precise relationship among the
K
-group and the local contributions is described as

\begin{split}&E_{\infty}^{0,-n}\cong{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})/F^{1}K^{-n},\\
&E_{\infty}^{1,-(n+1)}\cong F^{1}K^{-n}/F^{2}K^{-n},\\
&E_{\infty}^{2,-(n+2)}\cong F^{2}K^{-n}/E_{\infty}^{3,-n},\end{split}

(144)

or equivalently, the short exact sequences

\displaystyle\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>F^{1}K^{-n}@>{}>{}>{}^{\phi}K_{G}^{(\tau,c)-n}(T^{3})@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{0,-n}@>{}>{}>0,\\
0@>{}>{}>F^{2}K^{-n}@>{}>{}>F^{1}K^{-n}@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{1,-(n+1)}@>{}>{}>0,\\
0@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{3,-(n+3)}@>{}>{}>F^{2}K^{-n}@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{2,-(n+2)}@>{}>{}>0,\\
\end{CD}

(145)

in terms of the intermediate subgroups of
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})

\displaystyle{}^{\phi}K_{G}^{(\tau,c)-n}(T^{3})=F^{0}K^{-n}\supset F^{1}K^{-n}\supset F^{2}K^{-n}\supset F^{3}K^{-n}=E_{\infty}^{3,-(n+3)}

(146)

given by

\displaystyle F^{p}K^{-n}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}\left[\mathrm{res}:{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})\to{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)|_{X_{p-1}}-n}_{G}(X_{p-1})\right].

(147)

Here
{\rm res}
is the restriction homomorphism induced by inclusion

X_{p-1}\subset T^{3}
.
When the hierarchical extension problem (
145
) has a unique solution, the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K_{G}^{(\tau,c)-n}(T^{3})
is fixed as an Abelian group.
However, the extension problem (
145
) has multiple solutions in general.
In such cases, one can not evaluate the
K
-group only by the data
E_{\infty}^{n,-(n+p)}
(, although the rank of
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})
is the sum of those of
E_{\infty}^{p,-(n+p)}
).
A brute force approach to determine the
K
-group in such cases is finding an explicit formula of topological invariants compatible with the exact sequences (
145
) and collecting the “compatibility relation” among the topological invariants ((
99
) is an example of such relations).
Other exact sequences such as the Mayer-Vietoris and Gysin sequences in the
K
-theory can help us to determine the
K
-group.
Shiozaki et al. 2016
;
Shiozaki et al. 2017

Similarly, in two space dimensions, the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K_{G}^{(\tau,c)-n}(T^{2})
fits into the short exact sequences

\displaystyle\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>F^{1}K^{-n}@>{}>{}>{}^{\phi}K_{G}^{(\tau,c)-n}(T^{2})@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{0,-n}@>{}>{}>0,\\
0@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{2,-(n+2)}@>{}>{}>F^{1}K^{-n}@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{1,-(n+1)}@>{}>{}>0.\\
\end{CD}

(148)

Also, in one space dimension, the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K_{G}^{(\tau,c)-n}(S^{1})
obeys the short exact sequence

\displaystyle\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{1,-(n+1)}@>{}>{}>{}^{\phi}K_{G}^{(\tau,c)-n}(S^{1})@>{}>{}>E_{\infty}^{0,-n}@>{}>{}>0.\end{CD}

(149)

IV.1.1
On the exact sequences (
145
)

Here, we sketch why the exact sequences (
145
) hold true.
We employ the interpretation of the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})
as topological gapless states over
T^{3}
for symmetry class
(n+1)
.
(The sketch with the interpretation of the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})
as topological insulators for symmetry class
n
is parallel.)

Recall that
E_{\infty}^{p,-(n+p)}
has the following meaning:

•

E_{\infty}^{p,-(n+p)}
is the space of topological gapless states in
p
-cells for the symmetry class
(n+1)
which can extend to all the adjacent higher-dimensional cells without a singularity, and can not be trivialized by the creation of topological gapless points from any adjacent low-dimensional cells.

The definition (
147
) of
F^{p,-n}
implies that

•

F^{p}K^{-n}
is the space of topological gapless states over
T^{3}
for the symmetry class
(n+1)
which have a finite energy gap on the
(p-1)
-skeleton
X_{p-1}
.

Obviously, we have the injection
F^{p+1}K^{-n}\hookrightarrow F^{p}K^{-n}
, since the existence of a finite energy gap over
X_{p}
implies that there is also a finite energy gap over
X_{p-1}
, which leads to the sequence of inclusions (
146
).

The homomorphism
F^{p}K^{-n}\to E_{\infty}^{p,-(n+p)}
is defined by restricting gapless states into the
p
-cells.
Therefore, the group
{\rm Ker\,}[F^{p}K^{-n}\to E^{p,-(n+p)}_{\infty}]
represents gapless states with a finite energy gap on
X_{p-1}
that also have a finite energy gap over the
p
-cells.
On the other hand, the group
{\rm Im\,}[F^{p+1}K^{-n}\to F^{p}K^{-n}]
represents gapless states with a finite gap on
X_{p-1}
that also have a finite energy gap on
X_{p}
.
Since
X_{p}\backslash X_{p-1}
is the set of
p
-cells, two groups
{\rm Ker\,}[F^{p}K^{-n}\to E^{p,-(n+p)}_{\infty}]
and
{\rm Im\,}[F^{p+1}K^{-n}\to F^{p}K^{-n}]
are equivalent.

The finial step is to show that the homomorphism
F^{p}K^{-n}\to E^{p,-(n+p)}_{\infty}
is surjectve.
This follows from the definition of
E_{\infty}^{p,-(n+p)}
:
It is possible to extend a topological gapless state in an orbit
G/G_{D^{p}_{j}}\times D^{p}_{j}
of
p
-cells to the neighborhood of the orbit
G/G_{D^{p}_{j}}\times D^{p}_{j}
in the BZ
T^{3}
without affecting the Hamiltonian outside the neighborhood.
This means that there must be a representative topological gapless state in
F^{p}K^{-n}
for a topological gapless state of
E^{p,-(n+p)}
.

IV.2
Warm-up: real AZ class in two space dimensions

Figure 21:

[a]
A cell decomposition of the 2d BZ
with TRS
T:(k_{x},k_{y})\mapsto(-k_{x},-k_{y})
.
Here,
a^{\prime}=T(a),b^{\prime}=T(b),\alpha^{\prime}=T(\alpha)
represent equivalent
p
-cells.
The first differential
d_{1}^{1,{\rm even}}
.
The signs
\pm
in the circles represent the charges of the gapless Dirac point with chiral symmertry.
[c]
The second differential
d_{2}^{0,-1}
.
The band inversion at 0-cells creates a pair of Dirac points.

Let us compute the AHSS for
2d
systems with real AZ symmetry classes.
As the symmetry class for
n=0
, we consider the
2d
spinless systems with TRS.
I.e., class AI in the AZ classes.
We denote the TRS operator by
T
.
The symmetry group is
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{1,T\}
which acts on the
2d
BZ torus by
T:(k_{x},k_{y})\mapsto(-k_{x},-k_{y})
.
(Here, we denoted the group element of TRS by the same symbol
T
.)
The factor system is trivial
T^{2}=1
.
We use the cell-decomposition of the BZ torus shown in Fig.
21
[a].

From the formula (
137
), the
E_{1}
-page is the collection of the
K
-groups over
p
-cells with symmetry class shifted by
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We have the following
E_{1}
-page.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&\mathbb{Z}^{4}&\mathbb{Z}^{3}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}&0&0\\
{\rm D}&n=2&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}&\mathbb{Z}^{3}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&0&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&\mathbb{Z}^{4}&\mathbb{Z}^{3}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&0&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&0&\mathbb{Z}^{3}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{1}^{p,-n}&p=0&p=1&p=2\end{array}.

The first differential
d_{1}^{p,-n}:E_{1}^{p,-n}\to E_{1}^{p+1,-n}
is computed by the compatibility relation incorporating PHS as in

\displaystyle d_{1}^{0,0}=\begin{array}[]{cccc|c}\Gamma&X&Y&M&\\
\hline\cr 1&-1&0&0&a\\
0&0&1&-1&b\\
0&1&0&-1&c\\
\end{array},

\displaystyle d_{1}^{0,-4}=\begin{array}[]{cccc|c}\Gamma&X&Y&M&\\
\hline\cr 2&-2&0&0&a\\
0&0&2&-2&b\\
0&2&0&-2&c\\
\end{array},

\displaystyle d_{1}^{1,-2}=d_{1}^{1,-6}=\begin{array}[]{ccc|c}a&b&c&\\
\hline\cr 2&-2&0&\alpha\\
\end{array},

and
d_{1}^{p,-n}=0
for other
(p,-n)
s.
We should be careful about the PHS.
For
n=2
and
6
, the 1-cell
a
(
b
) changes to
a^{\prime}
(
b^{\prime}
) with the particle-hole transformation
C
.
Then, an occupied state
\ket{\phi}
at
a
is sent to an empty state
C\ket{\phi}
at
a^{\prime}
, which results in the nontrivial first differentials
d_{1}^{1,-2}
and
d_{1}^{1,-6}
.

Figure 22:

[a]
E_{1}
-page as topological gapless states.
[b] Relationship among the groups
E_{\infty}^{p,-n}
and AZ classes for gapped and gapless states.

Before moving on to the second differential
d_{2}
, it is worth understanding the first differential in terms of gapless Dirac points.
According to the meaning (II) of the
E_{1}
-page in Sec.
IV.1
,
E_{1}^{p,-n}
represents topological gapless points inside
p
-cells with the symmetry class
(n-p+1)
as illustrated in Fig.
22
[a].

E_{1}^{2,{\rm even}}=\mathbb{Z}
comes from that the chiral symmetry stabilizes a Dirac point in a 2-cell.
The first differentials
d_{1}^{1,{\rm even}}:E_{1}^{1,{\rm even}}\to E_{1}^{2,{\rm even}}
means how gapless Dirac points in the 2-cell are absorbed by the pair creation of the Dirac points from 1-cells.
For class BDI/CII, a time-reversal symmetric pair of Dirac points has the opposite charge because of the algebra
T\Gamma=\Gamma T
between the TRS
T
and the chiral symmetry
\Gamma
, whereas for class DIII/CI, the charge is the same because of the algebra
T\Gamma=-\Gamma T
.
See Fig.
21
[b].
This accounts for
d_{1}^{1,(-2)/(-6)}=(2,-2,0)
and
d_{1}^{1,0/(-4)}=(0,0,0)
.

Taking the cohomology group of
d_{1}
, we have the
E_{2}
-page

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&\mathbb{Z}&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}&0&0\\
{\rm D}&n=2&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}&\mathbb{Z}^{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&0&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&0&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&0&\mathbb{Z}^{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{2}^{p,-n}&p=0&p=1&p=2\end{array}.

In the
E_{2}
-page, the only second differential
d_{2}^{0,-1}:E_{2}^{0,-1}\to E_{2}^{2,-2}
can be nontrivial, and we find that
d_{2}^{0,-1}
is surjective:
We label 0-, 1-, and 2-cells as in Fig.
21
[a].
Notice that the even/odd parity of the class BDI
1d
winding number
w_{1d}[-a^{\prime}+a]
along the loop
-a^{\prime}+a
is the product of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariants at
\Gamma
and
X
as in
(-1)^{w_{1d}[-a^{\prime}+a]}=(-1)^{\nu(\Gamma)}(-1)^{\nu(X)}
.
In the same way, it holds that
(-1)^{w_{1d}[-b^{\prime}+b]}=(-1)^{\nu(Y)}(-1)^{\nu(M)}
for the
1d
winding number along the loop
-b^{\prime}+b
.
Therefore, the product
(-1)^{\nu}:=\prod_{{\bm{k}}\in\{\Gamma,X,Y,Z\}}(-1)^{\nu({\bm{k}})}
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
indicator to detect an odd number of class BDI Dirac points inside the 2-cell
\alpha
and
(-1)^{\nu}
is nothing but the second differential
d_{2}^{0,-1}
.

Alternatively, one can evaluate the second differential
d_{2}^{0,-1}
by the pair creation of the Dirac points from 0-cells.
The class BDI symmetry permits creating a pair of Dirac points from 0-cells that removes the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
remainder of
E_{2}^{2,-2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, the odd charges of Dirac points in the 2-cell.
(See Fig.
21
[c]).
Explicitly, such a process can be modeled as

\displaystyle H({\bm{k}})=(|{\bm{k}}-{\bm{k}}_{0}|^{2}-\mu)\tau_{z}+({\bm{k}}-{\bm{k}}_{0})\cdot\bm{n}\tau_{y},

\displaystyle T=K,\qquad C=\tau_{x},

(178)

around a 0-cell
{\bm{k}}_{0}
.
It is clear that when
\mu
passes zero, the band inversion occurs with the change of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant
(-1)^{\nu({\bm{k}}_{0})}
at the
0
-cell
{\bm{k}}_{0}
, resulting in a pair creation of of Dirac points direction perpendicular to
\bm{n}
.
This can contrast well with the case of class CII, where the pair creation of Dirac points from 0-cells should be doubly degenerate due to the TRS with Kramers degeneracy.
As a result, there are no class CII topological invariants at 0-cells (
E_{1}^{0,-5}=0
), which means that 0-cells can not be a new source of Dirac points, i.e., Dirac points arising from a 0-cell are recast as ones from 1-cells with continuous deformation.

Taking the cohomology of
d_{2}
, we arrive at the limiting page
E_{\infty}=E_{3}
,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&\mathbb{Z}&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}&0&0\\
{\rm D}&n=2&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}&\mathbb{Z}^{2}&0\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&0&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&0&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&0&\mathbb{Z}^{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{\infty}^{p,-n}&p=0&p=1&p=2\end{array}

The data
\{E_{\infty}^{0,-n},E_{\infty}^{1,-(n+1)},E_{\infty}^{2,-(n+2)}\}
approximate the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{-n}(T^{2})
in the sense of the exact sequences (
148
).
The relationship among columns of the
E_{\infty}
-page, AZ symmetry classes of
2d
bulk insulators and
2d
gapless states should be kept in mind, which is shown in Fig.
22
[b].
The dimension
p
of
E_{\infty}^{p,-n}
indicates the skeleton
X_{p}
on which the topological invariant is defined.
11
11

11

The band structures representing the group
E_{\infty}^{p,-n}
are insufficient to define the topological invariant, since
E_{\infty}^{p,-n}
represent only band structures inside
p
-cells that are trivial over the boundary of the
p
-cells.

The exact sequences (
148
) are recast as

\displaystyle{\rm AI}:

\displaystyle{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{0}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z},

(188)

\displaystyle{\rm BDI}:

\displaystyle 0\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{-1}(T^{2})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}\to 0,

(189)

\displaystyle{\rm D}:

\displaystyle 0\to\mathbb{Z}\to{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{-2}(T^{2})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}\to 0,

(190)

\displaystyle{\rm DIII}:

\displaystyle{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{-3}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3},

(191)

\displaystyle{\rm AII}:

\displaystyle{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{-4}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}_{2},

(192)

\displaystyle{\rm CII}:

\displaystyle{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{-5}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}^{2},

(193)

\displaystyle{\rm C}:

\displaystyle{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{-6}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z},

(194)

\displaystyle{\rm CI}:

\displaystyle{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}^{-7}(T^{2})=0.

(195)

These agree with the literature.
Schnyder et al. 2008

Especially,
E_{\infty}^{2,-6}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
corresponds to the Kane-Mele
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
topological invariant.
Kane and Mele 2005

The short exact sequences (
189
) and (
190
) show nontrivial group extensions.
12
12

12

Class BDI:
The
1d
winding numbers
w_{1d}^{x},w_{1d}^{y}
along the
k_{x}
and
k_{y}
directions, respectively, give the constraints on the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariants defined at 0-cells as
(-1)^{w_{1d}^{x}}=(-1)^{\nu(\Gamma)}(-1)^{\nu(X)}=(-1)^{\nu(Y)}(-1)^{\nu(M)}
and
(-1)^{w_{1d}^{y}}=(-1)^{\nu(\Gamma)}(-1)^{\nu(Y)}=(-1)^{\nu(X)}(-1)^{\nu(M)}
.
Class D:
The parity of the Chern number
C
is related to the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariants (Pfaffian invariants) at 0-cells as
(-1)^{C}=\prod_{{\bm{k}}\in\Gamma,X,Y,M}(-1)^{\nu({\bm{k}})}
.

We find that
{}^{\phi}K^{-1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}
and
{}^{\phi}K^{-2}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{3}
.

IV.3
E_{1}
-page for general symmetry

In this section we describe how to compute the
E_{1}
-page for general symmetry classes realized in lattice systems.
Let
G
be the symmetry group (magnetic point group) and
\phi,c:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
be the indicators for unitary/antiunitary and symmetry/antisymmetry, respectively.
We denote the symmetry operator for
g\in G
by
U_{g}({\bm{k}})
.
For the Hamiltonian
H({\bm{k}})
over BZ, the constraint relation is

\displaystyle U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})^{-1}=c(g)H(g{\bm{k}}),

(196)

\displaystyle U_{g}({\bm{k}})i=\phi(g)iU_{g}({\bm{k}}),

(197)

with
i
the imaginary unit.
The (magnetic) space group is specified by the point group action
p_{g}
on the real space and the possibly nonprimitive lattice translation
\{g|\bm{a}_{g}\}:\bm{x}\mapsto p_{g}\bm{x}+\bm{a}_{g}
associated with group elements
g\in G
.
Due to antiunitarity,
g\in G
with
\phi(g)=-1
acts on the momentum space by
{\bm{k}}\mapsto-p_{g}{\bm{k}}
.
To make the notation simple, we denote the group action on the momentum space by
{\bm{k}}\mapsto g{\bm{k}}
, i.e.
g=\phi(g)p_{g}
on
{\bm{k}}
.
The nonsymmorphic part of the factor system is described by a 2-cocycle
\bm{\nu}\in Z^{2}(G,BL)
, where
BL\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}
is the Bravais lattice translation group, which is given as

\displaystyle\{g|\bm{a}_{g}\}\{h|\bm{a}_{h}\}=\{e|\bm{\nu}_{g,h}\}\{gh|\bm{a}_{gh}\},

(198)

\displaystyle\bm{\nu}_{g,h}=p_{g}\bm{a}_{h}+\bm{a}_{g}-\bm{a}_{gh}\in BL.

(199)

Using
\bm{\nu}
, one can write down the factor system explicitly as

\displaystyle z_{g,h}e^{-igh\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})

(200)

where
z_{g,h}
represents the factor system of fundamental degrees of freedom under the point group.
For instance,
z_{g,h}\equiv 1
for spinless electrons, and
z_{g,h}\in\{\pm 1\}
for spinful electrons.
With the twisting
e^{i\tau_{g,h}({\bm{k}})}=z_{g,h}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}
, the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-0}_{G}(T^{3})
is defined.
For finite integer gradings
n>0
, the
K
-group
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})
is defined by adding chiral symmetries
\Gamma_{i}
,
\{\Gamma_{i},H({\bm{k}})\}=0
, with the following algebra:
Shiozaki et al. 2017

\displaystyle\{\Gamma_{i},\Gamma_{j}\}=2\delta_{ij},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}U_{g}({\bm{k}})=c(g)U_{g}({\bm{k}})\Gamma_{i},\qquad(g\in G).

(201)

One can show the Bott periodicity
{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-(n+8)}_{G}(T^{3})\cong{}^{\phi}K^{(\tau,c)-n}_{G}(T^{3})
.
In Appendix
C
, we summarize the list of factor systems for
n>0
much relevant to the condensed matter physics.

Let us move on to the
E_{1}
-page.
Let
X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset X_{2}\subset X_{3}=T^{3}
be
G
-filtration associated to a cell decomposition described in Sec.
III.1.1
.
From (
137
), the
E_{1}
-page is given by the space of representations incorporating TRS and PHS at
p
-cells

\displaystyle E_{1}^{p,-n}=\prod_{j\in I^{p}_{\rm orb}}{}^{\phi|_{D^{p}_{j}}}K^{(\tau,c)|_{D^{p}_{j}}-n}_{G_{D^{p}_{j}}}(D^{p}_{j}).

(202)

The problem is recast to finding irreps at a point in the
p
-cell
D^{p}_{j}
, which can be systematically solved by using the Wigner criteria
Inui et al. 2012
and the generalization thereof in the presence of PHS (see Appendix
A
).
The little group
G_{{\bm{k}}}
at
{\bm{k}}
is the subgroup of
G
so that
g\in G_{{\bm{k}}}
fixes the point
{\bm{k}}
, i.e.
G_{{\bm{k}}}=\{g\in G|g{\bm{k}}={\bm{k}}\}
.
The little group
G_{{\bm{k}}}
splits into the disjoint union of left cosets as

\displaystyle G_{{\bm{k}}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}}_{\rm unitary\ symmetries}

\displaystyle\sqcup\underbrace{aG_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}}_{\rm magnetic\ symmetries}

\displaystyle\sqcup\underbrace{bG_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}}_{\rm particle-hole\ symmetries}

\displaystyle\sqcup\underbrace{abG_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}}_{\rm magnetic\ particle-hole\ symmetries},

(203)

where
G^{0}_{{\bm{k}}}=\{g\in G_{{\bm{k}}}|\phi(g)=c(g)=1\}
is the subgroup of unitary symmetries,

a\in G

(\phi(a)=-c(a)=-1)
is a magnetic symmetry group element,

b\in G

(\phi(b)=c(b)=-1)
is a particle-hole symmetry group element,
and
ab\in G

(\phi(ab)=-c(ab)=1)
is a magnetic particle-hole symmetry group element.
In the Wigner criteria, we first determine irreps of the subgroup
G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}
.
The factor system of the little group
G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}
is given by

\displaystyle z_{g,h}^{{\bm{k}}}=z_{g,h}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}},\quad g,h\in G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}.

(204)

Let
\alpha,\beta,\dots,
be irreps of
G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}
with the factor system
z_{g,h}^{{\bm{k}}}
.
We introduce the following integer-valued quantities on each irrep

\displaystyle W^{T}_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{|G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}}z^{{\bm{k}}}_{ag,ag}\chi_{\alpha}((ag)^{2})\in\{\pm 1,0\},

(205)

\displaystyle W^{C}_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{|G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0}}z^{{\bm{k}}}_{bg,bg}\chi_{\alpha}((bg)^{2})\in\{\pm 1,0\},

(206)

\displaystyle W^{\Gamma}_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{0}}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{z^{{\bm{k}}}_{g,ab}}{z^{{\bm{k}}}_{ab,(ab)^{-1}gab}}\chi_{\alpha}((ab)^{-1}gab)\right]^{*}\chi_{\alpha}(g)\in\{1,0\},

(207)

which we call the Wigner indices.
Here,
\chi_{\alpha}(g\in G_{{\bm{k}}}^{0})
is the character of the irrep
\alpha
.
See Appendix
A
for the detail.
The datum
(W^{T}_{\alpha},W^{C}_{\alpha},W^{\Gamma}_{\alpha})
gives the emergent AZ class realized on the irrep
\alpha
, which determines the
E_{1}^{p,0}
terms.
Table
4
summarizes the relationship among Wigner indices
(W^{T}_{\alpha},W^{C}_{\alpha},W^{\Gamma}_{\alpha})
, emergent AZ classes, and corresponding band structures.

\hline\cr 0&1&0&{\rm D}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\begin{minipage}[56.9055pt]\scalebox{0.5}{\includegraphics[width,clip]{d_both}}
\end{minipage}\\
\hline\cr-1&1&1&{\rm DIII}&0&\begin{minipage}[56.9055pt]\scalebox{0.5}{\includegraphics[width,clip]{d3_both}}
\end{minipage}\\
\hline\cr-1&0&0&{\rm AII}&\mathbb{Z}&\begin{minipage}[56.9055pt]\scalebox{0.5}{\includegraphics[width,clip]{a2_both}}
\end{minipage}\\
\hline\cr-1&-1&1&{\rm CII}&0&\begin{minipage}[56.9055pt]\scalebox{0.5}{\includegraphics[width,clip]{d3_both}}
\end{minipage}\\
\hline\cr 0&-1&0&{\rm C}&0&\begin{minipage}[56.9055pt]\scalebox{0.5}{\includegraphics[width,clip]{d_both}}
\end{minipage}\\
\hline\cr 1&-1&1&{\rm CI}&0&\begin{minipage}[56.9055pt]\scalebox{0.5}{\includegraphics[width,clip]{bd1_both}}
\end{minipage}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Once we have determined the emergent AZ classes for the grading
n=0
, from the definition (
201
), the emergent AZ class and the band structure for other grading
n>0
follow the shift of symmetry classes as in

\displaystyle\to{\rm A}\to{\rm AIII}\to{\rm A}\to,

(208)

for emergent complex AZ classes, and

\displaystyle\to{\rm AI}\to{\rm BDI}\to{\rm D}\to{\rm DIII}

\displaystyle\qquad\to{\rm AII}\to{\rm CII}\to{\rm C}\to{\rm CI}\to{\rm AI}\to,

(209)

for emergent real AZ classes.

IV.4
Construction of higher differentials
d_{2}
and
d_{3}

As seen in Sec.
III
, the construction of the matrix
d_{1}^{p,-n}:E_{1}^{p,-n}\to E_{1}^{p+1,-n}
is straightforward.

d_{1}^{p,-n}
is determined by the compatibility relation (incorporating PHS in addition to TRS) among irreps.
On the other hand, there are no simple formulas for higher-differentials
d_{r}(r\geq 2)
.
However, as a case-by-case problem, they can be constructed through a model Hamiltonian.

As a preliminary step, consider the Hamiltonian description of the first differential
d_{1}^{0,-n}:E_{1}^{0,-n}\to E_{1}^{1,-n}
as in

\displaystyle H(k)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(k^{2}-\mu)&({\rm without\ PHS}),\\
(k^{2}-\mu)\tau_{z}&({\rm with\ PHS},\,C=\tau_{x}K).\\
\end{array}\right.

Here,
k
is the distance from the 0-cell we are considering.
The degeneracy of the irrep, if any, is implicit.
This Hamiltonian describes how gapless points appear on adjacent 1-cells when the irrep at the 0-cell passes the zero energy.
By identifying the irreps at the 0-cell and the gapless points on the 1-cells with elements of
E_{1}^{0,-n}
and
E_{1}^{1,-n}
, respectively, and taking into account the signs related to the orientations of these cells at the same time, the model Hamiltonian (
IV.4
) provides matrix elements of
d_{1}^{0,-n}
.

In a similar manner, we may construct the second differential
d_{2}^{0,-n}:E_{2}^{0,-n}\to E_{2}^{2,-(n+1)}
by using a model Hamiltonian.
Since
E_{2}^{0,-n}={\rm Ker\,}(d_{1}^{0,-n})
,
E_{2}^{0,-n}
represents sets of irreps at 0-cells that are smoothly extended to the 1-skeleton
X_{1}
without gapless states on it.
Some elements of
E_{2}^{0,-n}
can give rise to a gapless Dirac point in 2-cells, which is detected by
d_{2}^{0,-n}
.
To describe this process, we generalize Eq. (
IV.4
) to 2-dimensions.
Below, we construct model Hamiltonians for two examples.

Figure 23:
A
C_{4}
symmetric cell decomposition of 2-torus.
The figure shows one fourth of the BZ.

2d
class AI with
C_{4}
rotation symmetry (spinless electrons with the wallpaper group
p41^{\prime}
)—

The TRS
T
and 4-fold rotation
C_{4}
acts on the 2d BZ as
T:(k_{x},k_{y})\mapsto(-k_{x},-k_{y})
and
C_{4}:(k_{x},k_{y})\mapsto(-k_{y},k_{x})
, respectively.
The factor system is
T^{2}=1,C_{4}^{4}=1
and
TC_{4}=C_{4}T
.
We employ a
C_{4}
symmetric cell decomposition as in Fig.
23
.
A gapless Dirac point in the 2-cell is stabilized by a
\pi
-Berry phase introduced by the symmetry operator
TC_{2}
with
(TC_{2})^{2}=1
.
First, consider the creation of Dirac points from the
C_{4}
-invariant 0-cell, say the
\Gamma
point (or the
M
point).
To describe a band inversion, we consider a 2d generalization of Eq.(
IV.4
),
H=({\bm{k}}^{2}-\mu)^{2}
.
We also need another term in order to remove gapless point on adjacent 1-cells.
For this purpose, we add the
d_{x^{2}-y^{2}}
-density wave
\propto(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})
.
Since
(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})
gives a
(-1)
sign under
C_{4}
, to make the Hamiltonian
C_{4}
-symmetric, an orbital degrees of freedom compensating the
(-1)
sign is necessary.
Finally, the Hamiltonian for
d_{2}^{0,0}
around the
\Gamma/M
point is given by

\displaystyle H_{\Gamma/M}(k_{x},k_{y})=({\bm{k}}^{2}-\mu)\sigma_{z}+(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\sigma_{x},

\displaystyle T=K,\quad C_{4}=\sigma_{z},

(212)

where
\sigma_{i}
is the Pauli matrix in the orbital space.
This Hamiltonian describes the process that when
\mu
changes the sign the band inversion between the
C_{4}=1
and
c_{4}=-1
orbits occurs at
\Gamma/M
, and a quartet of Dirac points appear in the 2-cells.
Similarly, the creation of Dirac points from the
C_{2}
-invariant 0-cell (the
X
point) is described by

\displaystyle H_{X}(k_{x},k_{y})=({\bm{k}}^{2}-\mu)\sigma_{z}+(k_{x}+k_{y})\sigma_{x},

\displaystyle T=K,\quad C_{2}=\sigma_{z}.

(213)

These Hamiltonians specify the second differentials
d_{2}^{0,0}
from
\Gamma
,
M
and
X
to the 2-cell
\alpha
.

2d
class AII with
C_{4}
rotation symmetry (spinful electrons with the wallpaper group
p41^{\prime}
)—

It is instructive to compare the previous example with the case of class AII systems with
C_{4}
symmetry.
For spinful electrons, the factor system is given by
T^{2}=-1,C_{4}^{4}=-1
and
TC_{4}=C_{4}T
.
A gapless Dirac point in the 2-cell again has the quantized
\pi
-Berry phase protected by the symmetry
TC_{2}
with
(TC_{2})^{2}=1
.
However, in contrast to the class AI case, irreps at
\Gamma
,
M
are 2-dimensional, and thus a band inversion can create only an even number of Dirac points, which are not protected by the
\pi
-Berry phase.
Also, at the
X
point, no band inversion may occur because the irrep is unique.
Thus, we can conclude that
d_{2}^{0,0}
is trivial.

The third differential
d_{3}^{0,-n}:E_{3}^{0,-n}\to E_{3}^{3,-(n+2)}
may be constructed similarly.

d_{3}
describes how Weyl points are created in 3-cells by band inversion at 0-cells.
Here we give an example.

3d
class D (3d TRS-breaking superconductors)—

The PHS
C
acts on 3d BZ as
C:(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})\mapsto(-k_{x},-k_{y},-k_{z})
.
The factor system is
C^{2}=1
.
We set
C=\tau_{x}K
, where
\tau_{\mu\in\{x,y,z\}}
is the Pauli matrices in the Nambu space.
We consider Fig.
19
as a particle-hole symmetric cell decomposition.
Each high symmetry point
{\bm{k}}_{0}
of PHS is a fixed point of
C
, and the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-valued Pfaffian invariant
{\rm Pf\,}[\tau_{x}H({\bm{k}}_{0})]
is defined.
A band inversion at a high symmetric point may change the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant, and creates two Weyl points in the opposite direction at the same time.
This process is described by the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})=({\bm{k}}^{2}-\mu)\tau_{z}+k_{x}\tau_{x}+k_{y}\tau_{y},

\displaystyle C=\tau_{x}K,

(214)

where the origin of
{\bm{k}}
denotes a high symmetry point.
We conclude that the corresponding
d_{3}^{0,0}
is nontrivial.

IV.5
Indicators for gapless phases

From the definition of the differentials
d_{1}^{0,-n}
,
d_{2}^{0,-n}
and
d_{3}^{0,-n}
, it is clear that these kernels serve as the indicators detecting bulk gapless phases characterized by topological invariants at 0-cells.
Recall that
E_{1}^{0,-n}
is the data of topological invariants at 0-cells.
In
E_{1}^{0,-n}
, elements that satisfy the compatibility relation, namely
E_{2}^{0,-n}={\rm Ker\,}(d_{1}^{0,-n})\subset E_{1}^{0,-n}
, can glue together in the 1-skeleton
X_{1}
.
The Abelian group
E_{1}^{0,-n}/E_{2}^{0,-n}
describes bulk gapless phases with a gapess point inside a 1-cell.
In the same way,
E_{2}^{0,-n}/E_{3}^{0,-n}
and
E_{3}^{0,-n}/E_{4}^{0,-n}
describe bulk gapless phases thereof, which is summarized as follows:

•

E_{1}^{0,-n}/E_{2}^{0,-n}
is the indicator for the existence of a topological gapless point inside a 1-cell under the assumption that the Hamiltonian is gapped at all 0-cells.

•

E_{2}^{0,-n}/E_{3}^{0,-n}
is the indicator for the existence of a topological gapless point inside a 2-cell under the assumption that the Hamiltonian is gapped on the 1-skeleton
X_{1}
.

•

E_{3}^{0,-n}/E_{4}^{0,-n}
is the indicator for the existence of a topological gapless point inside a 3-cell under the assumption that the Hamiltonian is gapped on the 2-skeleton
X_{2}
.

IV.6
Case studies

We give four examples of the AHSS with TRS and/or PHS.

IV.6.1
2d
time-reversal symmetric spinless systems with two-fold rotation symmetry

Table 5:

The factor systems and the homomorphism
c:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for integer grading
n\geq 0
for spinless systems with TRS and
C_{2}
symmetry.

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l|c|l|ll}{\rm AZ}&n&z_{T,T},z_{C,C}&z_{T,g}/z_{g,T},z_{C,g}/z_{g,C}&z_{g,h}&c(g)\\
\hline\cr{\rm AI}&n=0&T^{2}=1&&&\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\rm BDI}&n=1&T^{2}=1,C^{2}=1&&&\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\rm D}&n=2&C^{2}=1&TC_{2}({\bm{k}})=C_{2}(-{\bm{k}})T&&\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\rm DIII}&n=3&T^{2}=-1,C^{2}=1&{\rm and/or}&C_{2}(-{\bm{k}})C_{2}({\bm{k}})=1&C_{2}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})C_{2}({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\rm AII}&n=4&T^{2}=-1&CC_{2}({\bm{k}})=C_{2}(-{\bm{k}})C&&\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\rm CII}&n=5&T^{2}=-1,C^{2}=-1&&&\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\rm C}&n=6&C^{2}=-1&&&\\
\cline{1-3}\cr{\rm CI}&n=7&T^{2}=1,C^{2}=1&&&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Let us consider, as the initial grading
n=0
, 2-dimensional spinless insulators with TRS
T
and the two-fold rotation symmetry
C_{2}
, where both the generators act on the 2d BZ torus as an inversion
T,C_{2}:(k_{x},k_{y})\mapsto(-k_{x},-k_{y})
.
The symmetry group is
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
.
A
G
symmetric cell decomposition is given by the same one in Fig.
21
[a].
The factor systems for
n=0
as well as
n>0
are summarizes as in Table.
5
.
At a 0-cell
{\bm{k}}_{0}\in\{\Gamma,X,Y,M\}
, the unitary subgroup introduced in (
203
) is
G_{{\bm{k}}_{0}}^{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{e,C_{2}\}
and the Wigner criterion for each irrep
C_{2}=\pm 1
is given by
W_{C_{2}=\pm 1}^{T}=1
, namely, the emergent AZ class is AI.
On 1- and 2-cells, the little group is
G_{{\bm{k}}}=\{e,TC_{2}\}
and the emergent AZ class is AI because
(TC_{2}({\bm{k}}))^{2}=1
.
On the basis of the emergent AZ classes realized in
p
-cells, we get the
E_{1}
-page

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&\mathbb{Z}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}^{2}&\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm D}&n=2&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&0&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&\mathbb{Z}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}^{2}+\mathbb{Z}^{2}&\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&0&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&0&0&0\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{1}^{p,-n}&\{\Gamma,X,Y,M\}&\{a,b,c\}&\{\alpha\}\\
&&p=0&p=1&p=2\end{array}

The first differential
d_{1}^{p,-n}
is straightforwardly given by the compatibility relation, i.e. how irreps at
p
-cells split into representations at adjacent
(p+1)
-cells.
We get the followings.

\displaystyle d_{1}^{0,0}=d_{1}^{0,-4}

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{|cc|cc|cc|cc|c}\Gamma&&X&&Y&&M&&\\
1&-1&1&-1&1&-1&1&-1&\\
\hline\cr 1&1&-1&-1&&&&&a\\
&&&&1&1&-1&-1&b\\
&&1&1&&&-1&-1&c\\
\hline\cr\end{array},

d_{1}^{0,-2}=d_{1}^{0,-1}=d_{1}^{0,0}
(mod 2), and other first differentials are zeros.
We have the
E_{2}
-page
E_{2}^{p,-n}={\rm Ker\,}(d_{1}^{p,-n})/{\rm Im\,}(d_{1}^{p-1,-n})

\displaystyle=\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&\mathbb{Z}^{5}&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{5}&0&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm D}&n=2&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{5}&0&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&0&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&\mathbb{Z}^{5}&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&0&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&0&0&0\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{2}^{p,-n}&p=0&p=1&p=2\\
\end{array}.

We find that the second differential
d_{2}^{0,0}
is nontrivial:

E_{1}^{2,-1}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
indicates the class AI point node in the 2-cell
\alpha
described by the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H(k_{x},k_{y})=k_{x}\sigma_{z}+k_{y}\sigma_{x},\qquad TC_{2}({\bm{k}})=K,

(239)

where
K
is the complex conjugate.
The point node (
239
) is stabilized by the
\pi
-Berry phase, and can be removed by the band inversion at
0
-cells described by the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle d_{2}^{0,0}|_{\Gamma,X,Y,M\to\alpha}:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}H(k_{x},k_{y})=(k^{2}-\mu)\sigma_{z}+{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{n}\sigma_{x},\\
T=\sigma_{z}K,\\
C_{2}=\sigma_{z},\end{array}\right.

where
\bm{n}
is a unit vector.
Similarly, the second differential
d_{2}^{0,-1}
is nontrivial:
The class BDI point node of
E_{1}^{2,-2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in the 2-cell
\alpha
is described as

\displaystyle H(k_{x},k_{y})=k_{x}\tau_{z}+k_{y}\sigma_{y}\tau_{y},

\displaystyle TC_{2}({\bm{k}})=K,\qquad\Gamma=\tau_{x}.

(243)

This point node is removed by a band inversion at 0-cells followed by creating a point node to the 2-cell
\alpha
described by the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle d_{2}^{0,-1}|_{\Gamma,X,Y,M\to\alpha}:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}H(k_{x},k_{y})=(k^{2}-\mu)\tau_{z}+{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{n}\sigma_{y}\tau_{y},\\
TC_{2}({\bm{k}})=K,\\
\Gamma=\tau_{x},\\
C_{2}=\sigma_{z},\end{array}\right.

with
\bm{n}
a unit vector.
We arrive at the
E_{3}(=E_{\infty})
-page

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&\mathbb{Z}^{5}&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}&0&0\\
{\rm D}&n=2&\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{5}&0&0\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&0&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&\mathbb{Z}^{5}&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&0&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&0&0&0\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&0&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{3}^{p,-n}&p=0&p=1&p=2\\
\end{array},

and the
K
-groups read as

\begin{split}&{}^{\phi}K^{-0}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}^{5},\\
&{}^{\phi}K^{-1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{5},\\
&0\to\mathbb{Z}\to{}^{\phi}K^{-2}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{5}\to 0,\\
&{}^{\phi}K^{-3}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})=0,\\
&{}^{\phi}K^{-4}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}^{5},\\
&{}^{\phi}K^{-5}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})=0,\\
&{}^{\phi}K^{-6}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z},\\
&{}^{\phi}K^{-7}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})=0.\end{split}

(257)

These are consistent with the
K
-groups computed in Ref.
Shiozaki and Sato 2014
, where
{}^{\phi}K^{-2}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}(T^{2})=\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{4}
.

We, here, illustrate the different interpretations (I)-(IV) of the
E_{1}
-page in Sec.
IV.1
for the group
E_{1}^{2,-2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Let us denote the subgroup
\{e,TC_{2}\}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{TC_{2}}
.
In the followings,
R_{s}
represents the classifying space of the emergent AZ class
s
.

(IV)—

E_{1}^{2,-2}\cong{}^{\phi}K^{-2}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{TC_{2}}}(D^{2}_{\alpha})\cong\pi_{0}(R_{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is the space of representations (incorporating PHS) of the
2
-cell
\alpha
with the symmetry class
n=2
(class D).

(I)—

E_{1}^{2,-2}\cong{}^{\phi}K^{-0}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{TC_{2}}}(D^{2}_{\alpha},\partial D^{2}_{\alpha})
is the classification of topological insulators on the 2-cell
\alpha
for symmetry class
n=0
(class AI) with the condition that the Hamiltonian is constant on the boundary
\partial D^{2}_{\alpha}
.
Using the isomorphism
{}^{\phi}K^{-0}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{TC_{2}}}(D^{2}_{\alpha},\partial D^{2}_{\alpha})\cong{}^{\phi}\widetilde{K}^{-0}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{TC_{2}}}(S^{2}_{\alpha})\cong\pi_{2}(R_{0})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we confirm that
E_{2}^{2,-2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
A model Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle H(k_{x},k_{y})=k_{x}\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}+k_{y}\sigma_{z}\tau_{x}+(m-\epsilon k^{2})\tau_{z},

\displaystyle TC_{2}({\bm{k}})=K.

(258)

(II)—
Using the isomorphism
{}^{\phi}\widetilde{K}^{-0}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{TC_{2}}}(S^{2}_{\alpha})\cong{}^{\phi}\widetilde{K}^{-1}_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{TC_{2}}}(S^{1}_{\alpha})\cong\pi_{1}(R_{1})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, we can interpret
E_{2}^{2,-2}
as the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
topological invariant defined on a circle
S^{1}_{\alpha}
enclosing the topological gapless point for symmetry class
n=1
(class BDI), which we already showed in (
243
).

Figure 24:
Interpretation of
E_{1}^{2,-2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as topological singular points in class D superconductors with
C_{2}
rotation symmetry.
The figure shows the corresponding topological point nodes in the 3-dimensional superconductor (
259
).

(III)—
Applying the bulk-boundary correspondence to the circle
S^{1}_{\alpha}
enclosing the topological gapless point in the interpretation (II), we have a gapless helical Majorana mode on
S^{1}_{\alpha}
with the symmetry class
n=2
(class D).
The existence of the topological gapless states on
S^{1}_{\alpha}
implies that there must be a singular point inside the circle
S^{1}_{\alpha}
.
Such a singular point appears on the surface of 3-dimensional gapless class D superconductors with
C_{2}^{z}
rotation symmetry.
A model Bogoliubov de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle H(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})=\left(\frac{k^{2}}{2m}-\mu\right)\tau_{z}+k_{y}\sigma_{z}\tau_{x}+k_{z}\tau_{y}

\displaystyle C=\tau_{x}K,\qquad C_{2}^{z}=\sigma_{z}

(259)

around the
\Gamma
point, where the gap function is
\Delta({\bm{k}})=(k_{y}\sigma_{x}+k_{z}\sigma_{y})i\sigma_{y}
.
The combined symmetry
CC_{2}:(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})\mapsto(k_{x},k_{y},-k_{z})
defines the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant
(-1)^{\nu}
on a plane
\Sigma=S^{1}_{xy}\times S^{1}_{z}
with
S^{1}_{xy}
a circle on the
k_{x}k_{y}
-plane and
S^{1}_{z}
the circle along the
k_{z}
-direction.
Shiozaki and Sato 2014

We see that the gapless points at
{\bm{k}}=(\pm\sqrt{2m\mu},0,0)
have nontrivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
charge with respect to
(-1)^{\nu}
, i.e., when a plane
\Sigma
passes the gapless point the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariant
(-1)^{\nu}
is flipped.
Between the two gapless points,
-\sqrt{2m\mu}<k_{x}<\sqrt{2m\mu}
, there appears the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
gapless helical Majorana state on the surface BZ.
(Here, the surface was defined to be perpendicular to the
z
-axis in order to preserve the
C_{2}^{z}
rotation symmetry.)
The projection of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
topological gapless points in bulk to the surface BZ becomes the singular points, each of which is described by
E_{1}^{2,-2}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Fig.
24
illustrates the relation between the surface singular points and bulk gapless points of the Hamiltonian (
259
).

IV.6.2
2d
time-reversal symmetric superconductors with the half lattice translation symmetry

Let us consider
2d
spinful time-reversal symmetric superconductors with the half lattice translation symmetry:
L_{\hat{x}/2}:(x,y)\mapsto(x+1/2,y)
.
We assume the gap function is odd under the half lattice translation,

L_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}})\Delta({\bm{k}})L_{\hat{x}/2}^{T}(-{\bm{k}})=-\Delta({\bm{k}})
,
while the normal Hamiltonian
{\cal E}({\bm{k}})
is invariant,
L_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}}){\cal E}({\bm{k}})L^{-1}_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}})={\cal E}({\bm{k}})
.
For the Nambu space of the BdG Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H({\bm{k}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\cal E}({\bm{k}})&\Delta({\bm{k}})\\
\Delta^{\dagger}({\bm{k}})&-{\cal E}^{t}(-{\bm{k}})\end{array}\right),

the half lattice translation is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}L_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}})&0\\
0&-L_{\hat{x}/2}^{*}(-{\bm{k}})\end{array}\right),

which yields the following symmetry for the BdG Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})\tilde{L}^{-1}_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}})=H({\bm{k}}).

(264)

Here
\tilde{L}_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}})
satisfies
C\tilde{L}_{\hat{x}/2}({\bm{k}})=-L_{\hat{x}/2}(-{\bm{k}})C
, because of the minus sign in Eq.(
IV.6.2
), which reflects the odd representation of the gap function under
L_{\hat{x}/2}
.
In general, the representation of the gap function under the symmetry group is encoded in the twist between PHS and unbroken symmetries in the Nambu space.
Once the twist is identified, only symmetry operators in the Nambu space are needed.
Therefore, we only use
\tilde{L}_{\hat{x}/2}
below, and we omit the symbol of “tilde” in the notation for simplicity.
It has been known that there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
invariant for this symmetry class.
Shiozaki et al. 2016

The symmetry group is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
, which is generated by the half lattice translation (
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
) and TRS (
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
).
The factor system reads
13
13

(glide plane).

on
the factor systems in Eq. (

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T^{2}=-1,\qquad C^{2}=1,\\

(DIII).

In class AII (
n=4
), the
E_{2}
-page is already the limiting page.
The exact sequences (
145
) imply that

\displaystyle{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}^{\tau-4}(T^{3})=\underbrace{\mathbb{Z}}_{\rm Filling\ number}+F^{1,-5},

(354)

where the group
F^{1,-5}
filts into the exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\underbrace{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{E_{\infty}^{2,-6}}\to F^{1,-5}\to\underbrace{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{E_{\infty}^{1,-5}}\to 0.

(355)

The AHSS alone can not determine
F^{1,-5}
.
Nevertheless, we find that either
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{2}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}
topological invariant should be defined on the
2
-skeleton.

Interestingly, there appears a
\mathbb{Z}_{8}
topological invariant in class DIII (
n=3
).
Since
E_{2}^{0,-4}=\mathbb{Z}
represents the filling number,
d_{3}^{0,-4}:E_{3}^{0,-4}\to E_{3}^{3,-6}
is trivial.
Then, the
K
-group fits into the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\underbrace{\mathbb{Z}}_{3d{\rm\ winding\ number}}\to{}^{\phi}K_{\mathbb{Z}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}}^{\tau-3}(T^{3})\to\underbrace{\mathbb{Z}_{8}+\mathbb{Z}_{4}+\mathbb{Z}_{2}}_{1d{\rm\ topological\ invariants}}\to 0.

(356)

The construction of the
\mathbb{Z}_{8}
invariant is similar to the screw
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
invariant discussed in Sec.
III.3.3
.
Let
q({\bm{k}})
be the off-diagonal part of the flattened Hamiltonian
{\rm sgn}[H({\bm{k}})]=\begin{pmatrix}0&q({\bm{k}})\\
q({\bm{k}})^{{\dagger}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}
in the basis so that the chiral operator is
\Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{pmatrix}
.
The symmetry of class DIII is written by
\sigma_{y}q({\bm{k}})^{*}=q(-{\bm{k}})^{{\dagger}}\sigma_{y}
,
S({\bm{k}})q({\bm{k}})=q(c_{4}{\bm{k}})S({\bm{k}})
,
\sigma_{y}S({\bm{k}})^{*}=S(-{\bm{k}})\sigma_{y}
.
The matrix
q(k_{z})
on the line
(k_{x},k_{y})=\Gamma
and
M
splits into the screw eigensectors as
q(k_{z})=q_{0}(k_{z})\oplus q_{1}(k_{z})\oplus q_{2}(k_{z})\oplus q_{3}(k_{z}),k_{z}\in[-\pi,\pi],
with the eigenvalues
e^{im\pi/4}e^{-ik_{z}/4}
,
m\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.
Moreover, at the high-symmetry points
(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})=(\Gamma,-\pi)
and
(M,-\pi)
on the zone boundary,
the TRS is closed inside the eigensector with
m=0
(and
m=2
), then, the Pfaffian
{\rm Pf}[\sigma_{y}q_{0}(-\pi)]
is well-defined.
The
\mathbb{Z}_{8}
invariant
e^{\pi i\nu/4},\nu\in\{0,1,\dots,7\}
is defined as

\begin{split}&e^{i\pi\nu/4}\\
&:=\frac{{\rm Pf}[\sigma_{y}q_{0}(\Gamma,-\pi)]\times\exp\left[\frac{3}{8}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}d\log\det q_{0}(\Gamma,k_{z})+\frac{2}{8}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}d\log\det q_{1}(\Gamma,k_{z})+\frac{1}{8}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}d\log\det q_{2}(\Gamma,k_{z})\right]}{{\rm Pf}[\sigma_{y}q_{0}(M,-\pi)]\times\exp\left[\frac{3}{8}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}d\log\det q_{0}(M,k_{z})+\frac{2}{8}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}d\log\det q_{1}(M,k_{z})+\frac{1}{8}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}d\log\det q_{2}(M,k_{z})\right]}\\
&\qquad\times\exp\left[\frac{1}{8}\int_{\Gamma\to M}d{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}\log\det q({\bm{k}},-\pi)\right].\end{split}

(357)

Using
{\rm Pf}(A)^{2}=\det(A)
, one can show
(e^{i\pi\nu/4})^{8}=1
.

superconductors in spinful systems with the space group
