&&&&&&I({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm BDI}&n=1&T^{2}=1&TC_{4}({\bm{k}})=-C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})T&TI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})T&&C_{4}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H({\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=1&CC_{4}({\bm{k}})=C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})C&CI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})C&&I({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm D}&n=2&C^{2}=1&CC_{4}({\bm{k}})=C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})C&CI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})C&&C_{4}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=-H({\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})\\
&&&&&&I({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm DIII}&n=3&T^{2}=-1&TC_{4}({\bm{k}})=C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})T&TI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})T&C_{4}(c_{4}^{3}{\bm{k}})C_{4}(c_{4}^{2}{\bm{k}})C_{4}(c_{4}{\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})=-1&C_{4}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H({\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=1&CC_{4}({\bm{k}})=-C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})C&CI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})C&I(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})=1&I({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm AII}&n=4&T^{2}=-1&TC_{4}({\bm{k}})=C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})T&TI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})T&I(c_{4}{\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})=C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})&C_{4}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=-H({\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})\\
&&&&&&I({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm CII}&n=5&T^{2}=-1&TC_{4}({\bm{k}})=-C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})T&TI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})T&&C_{4}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H({\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=-1&CC_{4}({\bm{k}})=C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})C&CI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})C&&I({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm C}&n=6&C^{2}=-1&CC_{4}({\bm{k}})=C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})C&CI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})C&&C_{4}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=-H({\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})\\
&&&&&&I({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm CI}&n=7&T^{2}=1&TC_{4}({\bm{k}})=C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})T&TI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})T&&C_{4}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H({\bm{k}})C_{4}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=-1&CC_{4}({\bm{k}})=-C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})C&CI({\bm{k}})=I(-{\bm{k}})C&&I({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})I({\bm{k}})\\
\hline\cr\end{array}}

In this section, we illustrate how the AHSS describes superconducting nodal structures.
Consider
2d
superconductors with TRS, inversion and 4-fold rotation symmetries.
We assume that the gap function obeys the
B_{g}
representation under the point group
4/m
.
Namely,

I({\bm{k}})\Delta({\bm{k}})I(-{\bm{k}})^{T}=\Delta(-{\bm{k}})
, and

C_{4}({\bm{k}})\Delta(k_{x},k_{y})C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})^{T}=-\Delta(-k_{y},k_{x})
.
In the Nambu space, the inversion and 4-fold rotation operators are given as

\displaystyle\tilde{I}({\bm{k}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}I({\bm{k}})&0\\
0&I(-{\bm{k}})^{*}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{4}({\bm{k}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}C_{4}({\bm{k}})&0\\
0&-C_{4}(-{\bm{k}})^{*}\end{array}\right),

by which we can determine the factor systems for class DIII (
n=3
).
According to Table
18
in Appendix
C
, the factor systems for other gradings
n
are summarized as in Table
8
.

Consider the cell decomposition in Fig.
23
.
Then the
E_{1}
-page is found to be

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&0+\mathbb{Z}^{2}+0&\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}^{2}+0+\mathbb{Z}^{2}&0&0\\
{\rm D}&n=2&0+\mathbb{Z}^{2}+0&\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&\mathbb{Z}^{2}+0+\mathbb{Z}^{2}&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&0+\mathbb{Z}^{2}+0&\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&\mathbb{Z}^{2}+0+\mathbb{Z}^{2}&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&0+\mathbb{Z}^{2}+0&\mathbb{Z}+\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&\mathbb{Z}^{2}+0+\mathbb{Z}^{2}&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{1}^{p,-n}&\{\Gamma,X,M\}&\{a,b\}&\{\alpha\}\\
&&p=0&p=1&p=2\\
\end{array}.

As we described in Sec.
IV.1
, the
E_{1}
-page
E_{1}^{p,-n}
can be regarded as the space of topological gapless points inside
p
-cells with the symmetry class
(n+1-p)
.
For example,
E_{1}^{2,-4}=\mathbb{Z}
means that the 2-cell
\alpha
can host stable
\mathbb{Z}
gapless points belonging to class DIII (i.e., gapless Majorana cones).

The compatibility relation (incorporating PHS) gives the first differentials.
The non-trivial ones are listed below.

\displaystyle d_{1}^{0,-n}=\begin{array}[]{|cc|c}X&&\\
I=1&I=-1&\\
\hline\cr-1&-1&a\\
1&1&b\\
\hline\cr\end{array},

\displaystyle d_{1}^{1,-n}=\begin{array}[]{|cc|c}a&b&\\
\hline\cr 2&2&\alpha\\
\hline\cr\end{array},

with even
n
.
It should be noted that
d_{1}^{1,-n}
for even
n
is non-trivial, even though the 1-cells
a
(
b
) and
a^{\prime}
(
b^{\prime}
) in the same
C_{4}
orbit have opposite directions with respect to the 2-cell
\alpha
.
This is because
C_{4}
for even
n
acts as antisymmetry,
C_{4}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=-H(-k_{y},k_{x})C_{4}({\bm{k}})
, as shown in Table
8
.
Occupied bands and empty ones are interchanged after the
C_{4}
-rotation, so the mismatch of the orientation between
a^{\prime}
(
b^{\prime}
) and
\alpha
is compensated in the compatibility relation.
As a result,
a
(
b
) and
a^{\prime}
(
b^{\prime}
) equally contribute to
\alpha
in
d_{1}^{1,0}
as
1+1=2
.
We get the
E_{2}
-page

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&\mathbb{Z}&0&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}^{4}&0&0\\
{\rm D}&n=2&\mathbb{Z}&0&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&\mathbb{Z}^{4}&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&\mathbb{Z}&0&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&\mathbb{Z}^{4}&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&\mathbb{Z}&0&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&\mathbb{Z}^{4}&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{2}^{p,-n}&p=0&p=1&p=2\\
\end{array}.

For example,
d_{1}^{1,-4}=(2,2)
represents a pair creation of Dirac points from 1-cells to the 2-cell
\alpha
in class DIII as shown in Fig.
26
[a].
Here, two Dirac points related by
C_{4}
rotation have opposite charges since

C_{4}
changes the chirality
\Gamma=iTC
.
The above process changes the total charges in the 2-cell
\alpha
by an even number, so only the parity of the charges is topologically stable.
In other words, we have
E_{2}^{2,-4}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Figure 26:
[a]
The first differential
d_{1}^{1,{\rm even}}
.
[b]
The second differential
d_{2}^{2,{\rm odd}}
.

Next, we ask if
E_{2}^{2,-n}
with even
n
is trivialized by the creation of Dirac points from 0-cells.
The corresponding second differential is
d_{2}^{0,-(n-1)}
, which maps
E_{2}^{0,-(n-1)}
to
E_{2}^{2,-n}
.
For even
n
,
E_{2}^{0,-(n-1)}
is nothing but
E_{1}^{0,-(n-1)}=\mathbb{Z}^{4}
, which counts (occupied) states with a definite eigenvalue of
C_{4}
, say
C_{4}=e^{i\pi/4}
, in the
I=\pm 1
sectors at
\Gamma
and
M
, respectively.
Since we have two independent eigensectors of
I
and two independent 0-cells
\Gamma
and
M
,
we need four independent integers
(n^{I=1}_{\Gamma},n^{I=-1}_{\Gamma},n^{I=1}_{M},n^{I=-1}_{M})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
.
The second differential arising from the
I=\pm 1
sector at
\Gamma
(or
M
) is modeled by the Hamiltonian with the
d_{x^{2}-y^{2}}
-wave type gap functions:

\displaystyle{\rm BDI}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}T=\tau_{x}K,\quad C=\sigma_{x}K,\\
H({\bm{k}})=({\bm{k}}^{2}-\mu)\sigma_{z}+\Delta(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\tau_{y}\sigma_{x},\\
\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle{\rm DIII}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}T=\sigma_{y}K,\quad C=\tau_{x}K,\\
H({\bm{k}})=({\bm{k}}^{2}-\mu)\tau_{z}+\Delta(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\sigma_{y}\tau_{y},\\
\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle{\rm CII}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}T=\tau_{y}K,\quad C=\sigma_{y}K,\\
H({\bm{k}})=({\bm{k}}^{2}-\mu)\sigma_{z}+\Delta(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\tau_{x}\sigma_{y},\\
\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle{\rm CI}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}T=\sigma_{x}K,\quad C=\tau_{y}K,\\
H({\bm{k}})=({\bm{k}}^{2}-\mu)\tau_{z}+\Delta(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\sigma_{x}\tau_{x},\\
\end{array}\right.,

with

\displaystyle C_{4}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\sigma_{z}}&0\\
0&-(e^{\frac{\pi i}{4}\sigma_{z}})^{*}\\
\end{pmatrix}_{\tau},\qquad I=\pm{\bf 1}_{4\times 4}.

(395)

These Hamiltonians describe the creation of Dirac points: when
\mu
changes the sign from negative to positive, a band inversion occurs at
\Gamma
(or
M
) and four equivalent Dirac points are pumped from
\Gamma
(or
M
) to the adjacent
C_{4}
-equivalent 2-cells (see Fig.
26
[b]).
This process gives the following nontrivial
d_{2}^{0,-(n-1)}
for even
n
,

\displaystyle d_{2}^{0,-(n-1)}=\begin{array}[]{|cc|cc|c}\Gamma&&M&\\
I=1&I=-1&I=1&I=-1&\\
\hline\cr 1&1&1&1&\alpha\\
\hline\cr\end{array}.

From this,
we arrive at the limiting page
E_{\infty}=E_{3}
as

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc|cccc}{\rm AI}&n=0&\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
{\rm BDI}&n=1&\mathbb{Z}^{3}+2\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
{\rm D}&n=2&\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
{\rm DIII}&n=3&\mathbb{Z}^{3}+2\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
{\rm AII}&n=4&\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
{\rm CII}&n=5&\mathbb{Z}^{3}+2\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
{\rm C}&n=6&\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
{\rm CI}&n=7&\mathbb{Z}^{3}+2\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
\hline\cr&E_{3}^{p,-n}&p=0&p=1&p=2\\
\end{array}.

As discussed in Sec.
IV.5
, a nontrivial element of
E_{2}^{0,-n}/E_{3}^{0,-n}
is the indicator of Dirac points in the 2-cell from topological invariants at
0
-cells.
For example,
E_{2}^{0,-3}/E_{3}^{0,-3}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
for class DIII relates the number of Dirac points
\nu_{\rm ind}
in the 2-cell to the numbers of occupied states at 0-cells as

\displaystyle(-1)^{\nu_{\rm ind}}=(-1)^{n^{I=1}_{\Gamma}+n^{I=-1}_{\Gamma}+n^{I=1}_{M}+n^{I=-1}_{M}},

(408)

where
n^{I=\pm 1}_{{\bm{k}}}
is the number of occupied states at
{\bm{k}}=\Gamma,M
with
C_{4}=e^{i\pi/4}
and
I=\pm 1
.
In general, the high symmetry points
\Gamma
and
M
are not located on the Fermi surface,
so we can neglect the gap function in the caluculation of the latter numbers under the weak pairing assumption.
As a result, the above equation is recast into

\displaystyle(-1)^{\nu_{\rm ind}}=(-1)^{n_{0,\Gamma}^{I=1}+n_{0,\Gamma}^{I=-1}+n_{0,M}^{I=1}+n_{0,M}^{I=-1}},

(409)

where
n_{0,{\bm{k}}}^{I=\pm 1}
is the number of bands at
{\bm{k}}
in the normal state that are below the Fermi level and with the eigen values
C_{4}=e^{i\pi/4}
and
I=\pm 1
.
For a single band superconductor

\displaystyle H({\bm{k}})=\varepsilon({\bm{k}})\tau_{z}+\Delta({\bm{k}})\sigma_{y}\tau_{y},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(-k_{y},k_{x})=\varepsilon(k_{x},k_{y}),

\displaystyle\Delta(-k_{y},k_{x})=-\Delta(k_{x},k_{y})

(410)

with the weak pairing assumption
|\Delta({\bm{k}})|\ll|\varepsilon({\bm{k}})|
at
{\bm{k}}=\Gamma,M
, the indicator is evaluated as

\displaystyle(-1)^{\nu_{{\rm ind}}}={\rm sgn}[\varepsilon(\Gamma)\varepsilon(M)].

(411)

If the normal state satisfies
\varepsilon(\Gamma)\varepsilon(M)<0
, there must be point nodes (=Dirac points), irrespective of details of
\varepsilon({\bm{k}})
and
\psi({\bm{k}})
if the gap function is in
B_{g}
representation.

V
E_{3}
pages for 230 space groups for class A and AIII

In Tables

,

,

,

,

,

, and
16
, we present the
E_{\infty}=E_{4}
pages for all the 230 space groups in the AZ classes A and AIII.
The
E_{3}
-pages are the same as
the
E_{2}
-pages since
d_{1}^{p,-n}=0
for any
p
and
n
in the abosence
of anti-unitary symmetry.
We have refered the database
Aroyo et al. 2006
to identify the nonprimitive
lattice translation of each group element.
In Tables
10
to
16
, we use the discrete torsion phase
\epsilon^{{\bm{k}}=\Gamma}(g,h)
defined by

\displaystyle U_{g}({\bm{k}}=\Gamma)U_{h}({\bm{k}}=\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{{\bm{k}}=\Gamma}(g,h)U_{h}({\bm{k}}=\Gamma)U_{g}({\bm{k}}=\Gamma),

(412)

for
g,h\in G
with
gh=hg
to specify the equivalence class of the group cocycle for projective representations for each space group with point group
G
.
(In Table
9
, we summarize the group cohomology
H^{2}(G,U(1))
for the point groups.)

is the order of group,

It is found that only the space group Nos. 2, 81, 82, 147, and 148 have a torsion group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in
E_{3}^{3,0}
.
Except for
E_{3}^{0,0}
and
E_{3}^{3,0}
in these space groups, the
E_{3}
-pages in Tables
10
to
16
coincides with the
E_{\infty}
ones:
For space groups in classes A and AIII, only
E^{0,0}_{3}
and
E_{3}^{3,0}

can be different
from those in the
E_{\infty}
page, since
d_{3}^{0,0}:E_{3}^{0,0}\rightarrow E_{3}^{3,-2}=E_{3}^{0,0}
can be nontrivial.
However, if
E_{3}^{0,0}=\mathbb{Z}
, one can argue that
d_{3}^{0,0}=0
as follows.
Because no space group symmetry is left as a little group in 3-cells,

E_{3}^{3,0}=\mathbb{Z}
corresponds to the trivial representation of space group.
Therefore,
d_{3}^{0,0}
relates a band inversion between
non-trivial irreps at 0-cells to the trivial representation in 3-cells,
but there is no such a homomorphism.
As a result, the
E_{3}
-page reduces to the
E_{4}
-page, which is the

E_{\infty}
-page in three dimensions.

For the space group Nos. 2, 81, 82, 147, and 148, on the other hand, we have checked that
d_{3}^{0,0}\neq 0
and the torsion
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
in
E_{3}^{3,0}
is trivialized by
d_{3}^{0,0}
.
This result implies representation enforced Weyl semimetals in these space groups.
We have also computed the
K
-group
K^{\tau-1}_{G}(T^{3})
directly via the Mayer-Vietoris sequence.

In the rest of this section, we present some technical details to compute the
E_{2}
-page.

For a given Bravais lattice
BL(\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3})
and a point group
G
, inequivalent classes of nonprimitive lattice translations
\bm{a}_{g}
associated with the point group action
g\in G
are classified by the group cohomology
H^{2}(G,BL)
, where
G
acts on the
BL
as a usual point group action on the real space.
A representative
\bm{\nu}\in Z^{2}(G,BL)
is given as

\displaystyle\{g|\bm{a}_{g}\}\{h|\bm{a}_{h}\}=\{e|\bm{\nu}_{g,h}\}\{gh|\bm{a}_{gh}\},

(413)

\displaystyle\bm{\nu}_{g,h}=p_{g}\bm{a}_{h}+\bm{a}_{g}-\bm{a}_{gh},\qquad(g,h\in G).

(414)

Here, one nonprimitive lattice translation
\bm{a}_{g}
is fixed for each group element of
G
.
In the
{\bm{k}}
-space, the point group
G
acts on the Bloch state projectively due to the two origins: (i) space group is nonsymmorphic; (ii) the fundamental degrees of freedom can obey a nontrivial projective representation of the point group
G
.
Let
\{\ket{{\bm{k}},i}\}_{i}
be a basis of Bloch states at
{\bm{k}}\in BZ
.
The point group acts on
\ket{{\bm{k}},i}
as

\displaystyle\hat{g}\ket{{\bm{k}},i}=\ket{g{\bm{k}},j}[U_{g}({\bm{k}})]_{ji},

(415)

\displaystyle U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}}),

(416)

where
(z_{g,h})\in Z^{2}(G,U(1))
is a factor system for a projective representation of the fundamental degrees of freedom.
At a fixed
{\bm{k}}\in BZ
, the equivalence class
[(z_{g,h}e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}})]
of the factor system belongs to the group cohomology
H^{2}(G_{{\bm{k}}},U(1))
, where
G_{{\bm{k}}}=\{g\in G|g{\bm{k}}={\bm{k}}\}
is the little group.
The equivalence class is immediately determined by the discrete torsion phase

\displaystyle\epsilon^{{\bm{k}}}_{g,h}:=\frac{z_{g,h}e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}}{z_{h,g}e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{h,g}}},

\displaystyle(g,h\in G_{{\bm{k}}},gh=hg).

(417)

There is a one-to-one correspondence between an element of group cohomology
H(G_{{\bm{k}}},U(1))
and a discrete torsion phase
\epsilon^{{\bm{k}}}
.
14
14

14

Let
G
be a finite group and
H^{2}(G,U(1))
the second group cohomology.
It holds that
H^{2}(G,U(1))\cong{\rm Hom}(M(G),U(1))
, where
M(G)
is the Schur multiplier.
Miller 1952

Once we get a representation at
{\bm{k}}\in BZ
, by using (
416
), the representation at other points
g{\bm{k}}\ (g\notin G_{{\bm{k}}})
connected by the point group is given by

\displaystyle U_{h\in G_{g{\bm{k}}}}(g{\bm{k}})=\frac{z_{h,g}e^{-ig{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{h,g}}}{z_{g,g^{-1}hg}e^{-ig{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,g^{-1}hg}}}U_{g^{-1}hg}({\bm{k}}).

(418)

Especially, the character
\chi^{g{\bm{k}}}(h)={\rm tr\,}U_{h}(g{\bm{k}})
compatible with the factor system at
{\bm{k}}
is given in this way.
Let
\chi^{{\bm{k}}}_{\alpha}(g)(g\in G_{{\bm{k}}},\alpha\in{\rm irreps})
be the irreducible characters with the factor system
z_{g,h}e^{-i{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}
.
For a given representation
\rho
with the same factor system, the irreducible decomposition is given as
\rho=\bigoplus_{i\in{\rm irreps}}n_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}
with the nonnegative integer

\displaystyle n_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{|G_{{\bm{k}}}|}\sum_{g\in G_{{\bm{k}}}}\chi^{{\bm{k}}}_{\alpha}(g)^{*}\chi^{{\bm{k}}}_{\rho}(g).

(419)

Combining Eqs. (
418
) and (
419
), we can determine the first differentials
d_{1}^{p,0}
.

Table 10:

E_{\infty}
pages for 230 space groups. The discrete torsion phase
\epsilon(g,h)\in\{+,-\}
specifies the algebra among elements in the point group (see eq.(
412
)).
The discrete torsion phases for spinless and spinful electrons are indicated with the subscripts “0” and “1/2”, respectively.
The torsion groups represented by
``(\mathbb{Z}_{2})"
in the columns of
E_{\infty}^{3,0}
are the
E_{3}^{3,0}
groups that disappear after taking the cohomology of the differential
d_{3}^{0,0}:E_{3}^{0,0}\to E_{3}^{3,-2}
.
I.e.,

VI
Conclusion and outlook

In this paper, we have studied the AHSS for twisted equivariant
K
-theory in the view of band theory.
As an application, we present the complete classification of topological invariants in A and AIII AZ symmetry classes for 230 space groups, summarized in Tables
10
to
16
.
We found that various torsion topological invariants appear even for symmorphic space groups.

As we have shown in Secs.
III
and
IV
, all the ingredients in the AHSS suitably fit into band theory.
The
E_{1}
-page has the data of irreps at
p
-cells, i.e., high-symmetry points, lines, planes, and volumes.
At the same time, the
E_{1}
-page can be thought of as the space of (i) topological insulators on
p
-spheres, each of which is defined by identifying the boundary of a
p
-cell to a point, (ii) topological gapless states in
p
-cells, and (iii) topological singular points in
p
-cells.
The differentials
d_{r}(r\geq 1)
in the AHSS represent the creation of topological gapless states (topological singular points) from
p
-cells to adjacent
(p+r)
-cells.
Especially, the first differential
d_{1}^{0,-n}:E_{1}^{0,-n}\to E_{1}^{1,-n}
is identified with the compatibility relation in the literature of band theory.
The
E_{r+1}
-page
(r\geq 1)
in the AHSS is defined as the cohomology of the
r
th differential
d_{r}
as
E_{r+1}^{p,-n}={\rm Ker\,}(d_{r}^{p,-n})/{\rm Im\,}(d_{r}^{p-r,-(n-r+1)})
, where
{\rm Im\,}(d_{r}^{p-r,-(n-r+1)})
is understood as the trivialization of topological gapless states in the
E_{r}
-page by
(p-r)
-cells, and
{\rm Ker\,}(d_{r}^{p,-n})
means a generalized compatibility relation for that the topological gapless states in
p
-cells can extend to adjacent
(p+r)
-cells without a singularity.
Here, a nontrivial
r
th differential
d_{r}^{0,-n}:E_{r}^{0,-n}\to E_{r}^{r,-(n+r-1)}
serves as the indicator of bulk gapless phases characterized by the high-symmetry points.
Iterating the cohomology of
d_{r}
yields the limiting page
E_{\infty}
.
Since topological gapless states represented by the
E_{\infty}
-page can not be trivialized by low-dimensional cells, an element of
E_{\infty}
-page is considered an anomalous gapless phase in the sense that it can not be realized as a stand-alone lattice system.
Moreover, the compatibility with higher-dimensional cells for topological gapless states of
E_{\infty}
implies that there must be a representative anomalous gapless phase in the whole BZ torus, which leads to the exact sequences (
145
) for the
K
-group.
In this sense, the
E_{\infty}
-page approximates the
K
-group.
From the bulk-boundary correspondence, the
E_{\infty}
-page approximates the classification of bulk gapped phases as well as anomalous gapless phases.

We close the paper by mentioning some future directions.

—
Although we showed the complete list of topological invariants for class A and AIII AZ classes for 230 space groups in Tables
10
to
16
, the explicit formulas of topological invariants remain undetermined for many space groups, which we left as the future work.

—
The quick construction of the higher-differentials
d_{r}(r\geq 2)
is not known yet.
Once an efficient method to derive the higher differentials is given, one can finish the computation of the
E_{\infty}
-page for all the magnetic space groups.
This may be an important step in completely classifying topological crystalline insulators and superconductors.

—
The
E_{\infty}
-page gives an approximation of the
K
-group in the form of exact sequences (
145
).
In some cases, the
K
-group as an Abelian group is not settled only by the
E_{\infty}
-page.
A complementary method is required to compute the exact sequences’ extension problem (
145
).

—
The relationship between bulk topological invariants and physical observables should be clarified.
Applying the “gauging crystalline symmetry” argued in Ref.
Thorngren and Else 2018
to the torsion topological invariants listed in Tables
10
to
16
is interesting.

—
Tables
10
to
16
indicates various torsion topological invariants in the column of
E_{2}^{1,0}
.
In addition to the meaning of the one-dimensional bulk class AIII invariant,
E_{2}^{1,0}
is interpreted as the spectral flow index for class A anomalous gapless spectra.
It is interesting to see the implication of torsion topological invariants in view of the interplay of the chiral anomaly and space group symmetry in the many-body Hilbert space.

Takuya Nomoto,
Akira Furusaki,
Takahiro Morimoto,
Ryo Takahashi and
Youichi Yanase
for helpful discussions.

K.G. is supported by JSPS KAKENHI (JP15K04871).
K.S. and M.S. thank the Yukawa Institute for Theoretical Physics at Kyoto University, where part of this work was done during the workshop “Novel Quantum States in Condensed Matter
2017” (NQS2017, YITP-T-17-01).

Appendix A
Wigner criteria

In this Appendix, we explain the Wigner criterion and its generalization in the presence of PHS.

Let
G
be a finite group and
\phi,c:G\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
the indicators for
unitary/antiunitary and symmetry/antisymmetry, respectively:

\displaystyle\phi(g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&(\mbox{unitary $g\in G$})\\
-1&(\mbox{antiuniary $g\in G$})\end{array}\right.,

\displaystyle c(g)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&(\mbox{symmetry $g\in G$})\\
-1&(\mbox{antisymmetry $g\in G$})\end{array}\right..

Let
z=(z_{g,h})\in Z^{2}(G,U(1)_{\phi})
be the factor system associated with a projective representation

\displaystyle z_{g,h}U_{gh}=U_{g}U_{h},\quad g,h\in G.

(424)

The factor system
z_{g,h}
satisfies the 2-cocycle condition

\displaystyle z_{g,hk}z_{h,k}^{\phi(g)}=z_{g,h}z_{gh,k}\qquad(g,h,k\in G).

(425)

Consider
G_{0}=\{g\in G|\phi(g)=c(g)=1\}
, the subgroup consisting of elements of unitary and symmetry, and let
\{\alpha,\beta,\dots\}
be irreps of
G_{0}
with the factor system

z|_{G_{0}}
.
The group
G
is the disjoint union of cosets
G=G_{0}\sqcup aG_{0}\sqcup bG_{0}\sqcup abG_{0}
, where
a,b,ab
are elements with
\phi(a)=-c(a)=-1,\phi(b)=c(b)=-1
, and
\phi(ab)=-c(ab)=1
, respectively.

Let
\ket{i},i=1,\dots,{\rm dim\,}\alpha
, be a basis of the irrep
\alpha
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\hat{g}\ket{i}=\ket{j}[D_{g}^{\alpha}]_{ji},

\displaystyle D_{g}^{\alpha}D_{h}^{\alpha}=z_{g,h}D^{\alpha}_{gh},\qquad g,h\in G_{0}.

(426)

Then, we formally introduce a conjugate representation
\hat{a}\ket{i}
as

\displaystyle\hat{g}(\hat{a}\ket{i})=(\hat{a}\ket{j})\frac{z_{g,a}}{z_{a,a^{-1}ga}}[D^{\alpha}_{a^{-1}ga}]^{*}_{ji}

(427)

for
g\in G_{0}
.
Here we have used the relations

\displaystyle\widehat{a^{-1}ga}|i\rangle=|j\rangle[D^{\alpha}_{a^{-1}ga}]_{ji},

\displaystyle\hat{a}\widehat{a^{-1}ga}=z_{a,a^{-1}ga}\widehat{ga}=\frac{z_{a,a^{-1}ga}}{z_{g,a}}\hat{g}\hat{a},

(428)

where the coefficient in the latter equation is determined by
(
424
).
Now we ask if
\hat{a}\ket{i}
is unitary equivalent to
\ket{i}
, and
if so,
\hat{a}|i\rangle
and
|i\rangle
are a Kramers pair or not.
First, let us assume that
\hat{a}\ket{i}
is unitary equivalent to
\ket{i}
, i.e. there exists a unitary matrix
V
such that

\displaystyle\frac{z_{g,a}}{z_{a,a^{-1}ga}}D_{a^{-1}ga}^{\alpha*}=V^{{\dagger}}D^{\alpha}_{g}V,\qquad(g\in G_{0}).

(429)

This relation leads to

\displaystyle D^{\alpha}_{g}(VV^{*})^{-1}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}}

\displaystyle=D^{\alpha}_{g}V^{T}V^{\dagger}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{aga^{-1},a}}{z_{a,g}}V^{T}D^{\alpha*}_{aga^{-1}}V^{\dagger}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{aga^{-1},a}z_{a,aga^{-1}}}{z_{a,g}z_{a^{2}ga^{-2},a}}V^{T}V^{\dagger}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}ga^{-2}}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}},

(430)

which is recast into

\displaystyle D^{\alpha}_{g}(VV^{*})^{-1}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{aga^{-1},a}z_{a,aga^{-1}}z_{a^{2}ga^{-2},a^{2}}}{z_{a,g}z_{a^{2}ga^{-2},a}z_{a^{2},g}}(VV^{*})^{-1}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}}D^{\alpha}_{g}.

(431)

From the 2-cocycle condition (
425
),
the prefactor in the r.h.s. of (
431
) is unity,
and thus we have

D^{\alpha}_{g}(VV^{*})^{-1}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}}=(VV^{*})^{-1}D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}}D^{\alpha}_{g}
.
Since the irrep
\alpha
is irreducible, the Schur’s lemma yields that
(VV^{*})^{-1}D_{a^{2}}^{\alpha}=\xi
with
\xi
a constant.
Substituting this into Eq. (
429
) with
g=a^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{z_{a^{2},a}}{z_{a,a^{2}}}V^{*}V/\xi=V^{\dagger}(VV^{*})\xi V,

(432)

which leads to
z_{a,a}\xi=\pm 1
, using the 2-cocycle condition
z_{a,a}^{-1}z_{a^{2},a}^{-1}z_{a,a^{2}}z_{a,a}^{-1}=1
.
Now introduce a new basis
\widetilde{\ket{i}}=(\hat{a}\ket{j})V^{{\dagger}}_{ji}
obeying the same representation of
\ket{i}
, i.e.
\hat{g}\widetilde{\ket{i}}=\widetilde{\ket{j}}[D^{\alpha}_{g}]_{ji}
.
Taking the same transformation twice, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{(\widetilde{\ket{i}})}

\displaystyle=(\hat{a}\widetilde{\ket{j}})V^{\dagger}_{ji}

\displaystyle=(\hat{a}\hat{a}\ket{k})V^{T}_{kj}V^{\dagger}_{ji}

\displaystyle=z_{a,a}(\widehat{a^{2}}\ket{k})V^{T}_{kj}V^{\dagger}_{ji}

\displaystyle=z_{a,a}\ket{l}[D^{\alpha}_{a^{2}}]_{lk}V^{T}_{kj}V^{\dagger}_{ji}

\displaystyle=z_{a,a}\xi\ket{i}

(433)

Therefore, when
z_{a,a}\xi=-1(+1)
,
\ket{i}
and
\hat{a}\ket{i}
are (not) a Kramers pair.

The sign
\xi z_{a,a}
is computed as follows.
Using the orthogonality condition
\sum_{g\in G_{0}}[D^{\alpha}_{g}]^{*}_{ij}[D^{\beta}_{g}]_{kl}=\frac{|G_{0}|}{{\rm dim}(\alpha)}\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}\delta^{\alpha,\beta}
between irreps
\alpha
and
\beta
,
we find that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{0}}\left[\frac{z_{g,a}}{z_{a,a^{-1}ga}}D^{\alpha*}_{a^{-1}ga}\right]^{*}D^{\alpha}_{g}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{\xi}{{\rm dim}(\alpha)}D_{a^{2}}^{\alpha{\dagger}}&(\ket{i}\ {\rm and\ }\hat{a}\ket{i}\ {\rm are\ equivalent}),\\
0&(\ket{i}\ {\rm and\ }\hat{a}\ket{i}\ {\rm are\ inequivalent}).\end{array}\right.

Moreover, using the 2-cocycle condition (
425
), we have

\displaystyle W^{T}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{0}}z_{ag,ag}\chi_{\alpha}((ag)^{2})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}z_{a,a}\xi=\pm 1&(\ket{i}\ {\rm and\ }\hat{a}\ket{i}\ {\rm are\ equivalent}),\\
0&(\ket{i}\ {\rm and\ }\hat{a}\ket{i}\ {\rm are\ inequivalent}),\end{array}\right.

where
\chi_{\alpha}(g\in G_{0})={\rm tr\,}D^{\alpha}_{g}
is the character of the irrep
\alpha
.

In the same way, for the group element
b\in G
(a representative element of PHS), we define

\displaystyle W^{C}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{0}}z_{bg,bg}\chi_{\alpha}((bg)^{2})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\pm 1&(\ket{i}\ {\rm and\ }\hat{b}\ket{i}\ {\rm are\ equivalent}),\\
0&(\ket{i}\ {\rm and\ }\hat{b}\ket{i}\ {\rm are\ inequivalent}).\end{array}\right.

We also introduce for
ab\in G
(a representative element of unitary PHS) the bit-valued quantity

\displaystyle W^{\Gamma}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{|G_{0}|}\sum_{g\in G_{0}}\left[\frac{z_{g,ab}}{z_{ab,(ab)^{-1}gab}}\chi_{\alpha}((ab)^{-1}gab)\right]^{*}\chi_{\alpha}(g)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&(\ket{i}\ {\rm and\ }\hat{a}b\ket{i}\ {\rm are\ equivalent}),\\
0&(\ket{i}\ {\rm and\ }\hat{a}b\ket{i}\ {\rm are\ inequivalent}).\end{array}\right.

Using the datum
(W^{T}_{\alpha},W^{C}_{\alpha},W^{\Gamma}_{\alpha})
, one can determine the emergent AZ symmetry class of the irrep
\alpha
as in Table.
4
.

Appendix B
On the classification of singular points

This Appendix presents the classification of topologically stable singular points strictly inside a
p
-cell (
p\geq 1
).
Here, a singular point means a point in the
k
-space where the Hamiltonian is not single-valued.
Let us focus on a
p
-cell
D^{p}
, where a little group
G_{D^{p}}
and a factor system on
D^{p}
are given.
According to Table
4
, the Wigner indices (
205
-
207
) determines the emergent AZ class realized in the
p
-cell
D^{p}
.
Let
s
be the integer indicating the emergent AZ class as shown in Table
17
.
We observe that a singular point inside the
p
-cell
D^{p}
should be the end point of a gapless Dirac line described by the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\rm gapless}=\sum_{\mu=1}^{p-1}k_{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}.

(442)

The gapless points of this Hamiltonian (
442
) form a straight line along the
k_{p}
-axis.
To have the topological gapless state (
442
), there should exist the topological invariant on the
(p-2)
-dimensional sphere surrounding the Dirac line of (
442
), which is classified by the homotopy group
\pi_{p-2}(R_{s})=\pi_{0}(R_{s+p-2})
(
\pi_{p-2}(C_{s})=\pi_{0}(C_{s+p-2})
) for emergent real (complex) AZ classes.
Here,
R_{s}
(
C_{s}
) is the classifying space of the real (complex) AZ class
s
.
Kitaev 2009

The homotopy groups for
1
-,
2
- and
3
-cells is summarized in Table
17
.
(The same formula holds true for 1-cells.)
Since the singular point is the end point of the massless Dirac line, the classification of stable singular points is the same as that for stable massless Dirac lines, which implies that Table
17
also gives the classification of the singular points.
In fact, using the massless Dirac line (
442
), we have an explicit model for the
p
-dimensional Hamiltonian describing the singular point

\displaystyle H_{\rm singular}={\rm Im\,}\ln\left[k_{p}+i\sum_{\mu=1}^{p-1}k_{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}\right].

(443)

We see that the Hamiltonian (
443
) is recast as the Dirac line
H_{\rm singular}\sim\sum_{\mu=1}^{p-1}k_{\mu}\gamma_{\mu}
on the
k_{p}
-axis with
k_{p}>0
, whereas the Hamiltonian (
443
) has a finite energy gap as
H_{\rm singular}\sim\pm\pi
on the
k_{p}
-axis with
k_{p}<0
.

Table 17:
The classification of singular points inside
p
-cells.
The emergent AZ class
s
is obtained by the Wigner test (
205
-
207
).

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\mbox{Emergent AZ class}&s&p=1&p=2&p=3\\
\hline\cr\mbox{A}&0&0&\mathbb{Z}&0\\
\mbox{AIII}&1&\mathbb{Z}&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
\hline\cr\mbox{AI}&0&0&\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\mbox{BDI}&1&\mathbb{Z}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}\\
\mbox{D}&2&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&0\\
\mbox{DIII}&3&\mathbb{Z}_{2}&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
\mbox{AII}&4&0&\mathbb{Z}&0\\
\mbox{CII}&5&\mathbb{Z}&0&0\\
\mbox{C}&6&0&0&0\\
\mbox{CI}&7&0&0&\mathbb{Z}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Appendix C
Factor systems

In this Appendix, we tabulate factor systems appearing in electronic systems.

C.1
Time-reversal symmetric spinless/spinful systems

A peculiarity in electron systems is that the factor system (at the
\Gamma
point) is determined by a spin representation of continuum rotation group
O(3)
and TRS.
Let
G\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
be the symmetry group composed by the point group
G
and the TRS
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
.
For spin integer systems, the factor system is trivial
z_{g,h}\equiv 1
for
g,h\in G\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
.
For spin half integer systems, the factor system obeys (i)
T^{2}=-1
, (ii)
TU_{g}=U_{g}T(g\in G)
, and the factor system
z_{g,h}
in
U_{g}U_{h}=z_{g,h}U_{gh}(g,h\in G)
follows the
Pin_{-}(3)
group (spin
1/2
representation of the
O(3)
group), which is known as the double group in the literature.
Inui et al. 2012

It should be noted that the inversion
I
always commutes with other symmetry group operators, since the inversion does not affect the internal degrees of freedom of spin.
15
15

15

Mathematically, the inversion operator can anticommutes with other symmetry operators.

The factor system in the whole BZ is summarized as

\displaystyle{\rm AI/AII}:\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T^{2}=\pm 1,\\
U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}}),\\
TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T,\end{array}\right.

where
T=U_{T}K
with
K
the complex conjugation,
\pm 1
is for spinless/spinful, and
z_{g,h}
is the factor system of the point group.
We have specified the data
\phi,c
of elements by symbols
T
and
U_{g}
, i.e.
\phi(T)=-c(T)=-1,\phi(g)=c(g)=1(g\in G)
.
By adding the chiral symmetries with the rule (
201
), the factor systems for other AZ classes are given, which are summarized in Table (
18
) (with setting
e^{i\theta_{g}}=1
and
z_{g,h}=1
for AI and
z_{g,h}
being the spin half integer representation for AII).
For a derivation of Table (
18
), see the next section.

Table 18:
The factor systems for time-reversal symmetric systems.
In the table,
T
and
C
are TRS and PHS, respectively,
z_{g,h}
represents the factor system of the point group, and
e^{i\theta_{g}}
takes values in
\{\pm 1\}
.

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l|l|l|l}{\rm AZ}&n&z_{T,T},z_{C,C}&z_{T,g}/z_{g,T},z_{C,g}/z_{g,C}&z_{g,h}&c(g)\\
\hline\cr{\rm AI}&n=0&T^{2}=1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&&&=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})\\
\hline\cr{\rm BDI}&n=1&T^{2}=1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})\\
\hline\cr{\rm D}&n=2&C^{2}=1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&&&=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})\\
\hline\cr{\rm DIII}&n=3&T^{2}=-1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})\\
\hline\cr{\rm AII}&n=4&T^{2}=-1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&&&=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})\\
\hline\cr{\rm CII}&n=5&T^{2}=-1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=-1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})\\
\hline\cr{\rm C}&n=6&C^{2}=-1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&&&=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})&\\
\hline\cr{\rm CI}&n=7&T^{2}=1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=-1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

C.2
Time-reversal invariant superconductors

In this section we formulate the factor system for spinful time-reversal invariant superconductors (i.e. class DIII) with space group symmetry.
The factor systems for spinless time-reversal invariant superconductors (class BDI) and spinful time-reversal invariant superconductors with
SU(2)
spin rotation symmetry (class CI) are constructed similarly.
A peculiarity of superconducting systems is that the factor system
depends on representations of the gap function under the point group.

Consider the BdG Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H({\bm{k}})=\begin{pmatrix}{\cal E}({\bm{k}})&\Delta({\bm{k}})\\
\Delta({\bm{k}})^{{\dagger}}&-{\cal E}^{T}(-{\bm{k}})\end{pmatrix},

(447)

where
{\cal E}({\bm{k}})
is the normal Hamiltonian and
\Delta({\bm{k}})
is the
gap function.
We assume that the normal part is invariant under space group symmetry

G
and TRS,

\displaystyle U_{g}({\bm{k}}){\cal E}({\bm{k}})U^{\dagger}_{g}({\bm{k}})={\cal E}(g{\bm{k}}),\quad T{\cal E}({\bm{k}})T^{-1}=H(-{\bm{k}}),

(448)

with the factor system

\displaystyle U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot{\bm{\nu}}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}}),\quad T^{2}=-1,\quad TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T.

(449)

We also assume that the gap function
\Delta({\bm{k}})
does not break TRS,
so by taking a proper phase of
T
, we have

\displaystyle T\Delta({\bm{k}})T^{T}=\Delta(-{\bm{k}}),

(450)

without changing the factor system Eq. (
449
).
In order for the gap function
\Delta({\bm{k}})
to keep the space group
symmetry
G
, it should be a 1-dimensional representation of
G
,

\displaystyle U_{g}({\bm{k}})\Delta({\bm{k}})U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})^{T}=e^{i\theta_{g}}\Delta(g{\bm{k}}),\qquad e^{i(\theta_{g}+\theta_{h})}=e^{i\theta_{gh}},\qquad g,h\in G.

(451)

While the original space group symmetry is
broken when
e^{i\theta_{g}}\neq 1
,
we can restore it by combining
U(1)
gauge symmetry.
However, not all 1
d
represenations are compatible with TRS:
Applying

T^{-1}U_{g}^{-1}(-{\bm{k}})TU_{g}({\bm{k}})

to the gap function, we have

\displaystyle[T^{-1}U_{g}^{-1}(-{\bm{k}})TU_{g}({\bm{k}})]\Delta({\bm{k}})[T^{-1}U_{g}^{-1}({\bm{k}})TU_{g}(-{\bm{k}})]^{T}

\displaystyle=T^{-1}U_{g}^{-1}(-{\bm{k}})TU_{g}({\bm{k}})\Delta({\bm{k}})U_{g}^{T}(-{\bm{k}})T^{T}U_{g}^{*}({\bm{k}})[T^{-1}]^{T}

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta_{g}}T^{-1}U_{g}^{-1}(-{\bm{k}})T\Delta(g{\bm{k}})T^{T}U_{g}^{*}({\bm{k}})[T^{-1}]^{T}

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta_{g}}T^{-1}U_{g}^{-1}(-{\bm{k}})\Delta(-g{\bm{k}})U_{g}^{*}({\bm{k}})[T^{-1}]^{T}

\displaystyle=e^{2i\theta_{g}}T^{-1}\Delta(-{\bm{k}})[T^{-1}]^{T}

\displaystyle=e^{2i\theta_{g}}\Delta({\bm{k}}).

(452)

On the other hand, the factor system in Eq.(
449
)
implies
T^{-1}U_{g}^{-1}(-{\bm{k}})TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=1
, leading to
e^{2i\theta_{g}}=1
.
Therefore, only when
e^{i\theta_{g}}=\pm 1
, TRS and the space group
G
can coexist.

For the BdG Hamiltonian
H({\bm{k}})
, the space group symmetry
G
and TRS is given as

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})\widetilde{U}_{g}({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}}),\quad\widetilde{T}H({\bm{k}}){\widetilde{T}}^{-1}=H(-{\bm{k}}),

(453)

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{g}({\bm{k}}):=\begin{pmatrix}U_{g}({\bm{k}})&0\\
0&e^{-i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})^{*}\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad\widetilde{T}:=\begin{pmatrix}T&0\\
0&(T^{-1})^{T}\end{pmatrix}.

(454)

Since
T=U_{T}K
with a unitary matrix
U_{T}
and
the complex conjugation operator
K
,
\widetilde{T}
is also written as

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}=\begin{pmatrix}U_{T}&0\\
0&U_{T}^{*}\end{pmatrix}K.

(455)

The BdG Hamiltonian also has the inherent PHS

\displaystyle CH({\bm{k}})C^{-1}=-H(-{\bm{k}}),\quad C=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}K.

(456)

From the above relations, we can identify the factor system among TRS,
PHS, and point group symmetry:

\displaystyle{\rm DIII}:\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T^{2}=-1,\qquad C^{2}=1,\qquad U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}}),\\
TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T,\qquad CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C,\qquad e^{i\theta_{g}}\in\{\pm 1\},\\
TH({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})T,\qquad CH({\bm{k}})=-H(-{\bm{k}})C,\qquad U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}}).\end{array}\right.,

where we have omitted the tilde in
T
and
U_{g}
for the simplicity of
notation.
This gives the factor system for
n=3
in Table
18
.

By imposing an additional chiral symmetry
\Gamma_{1}
on
H({\bm{k}})
,
we can obtain the factor system
for
n=4
(class AII) in Table
18
.
For this purpose, we use the chiral operator
\Gamma=iTC
in the factor
system of class DIII, instead of
PHS: Assuming that
\Gamma_{1}
satisfies Eq.(
201
), we have

\displaystyle{\rm DIII}\to{\rm AII}:\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gamma^{2}=1,\qquad T^{2}=-1,\qquad T\Gamma=-\Gamma T,\\
U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}}),\\
TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T,\qquad\Gamma U_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})\Gamma,\qquad e^{i\theta_{g}}\in\{\pm 1\},\\
\Gamma_{1}^{2}=1,\qquad\Gamma\Gamma_{1}=-\Gamma_{1}\Gamma,\qquad T\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{1}T,\qquad\Gamma_{1}U_{g}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})\Gamma_{1}.\end{array}\right.

This algebra is indeed that in class AII as follows.
Without loss of generality, the symmetry operators and the Hamiltonian
H({\bm{k}})
can be written as

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Gamma=\sigma_{x}\otimes{\bf 1},\qquad\Gamma_{1}=\sigma_{z}\otimes{\bf 1},\qquad T=\sigma_{z}\otimes T^{\prime},\\
U_{g}({\bm{k}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma_{0}\otimes U^{\prime}_{g}({\bm{k}})&(e^{i\theta_{g}}=1)\\
\sigma_{z}\otimes U^{\prime}_{g}({\bm{k}})&(e^{i\theta_{g}}=-1)\end{array}\right.,\\
H({\bm{k}})=\sigma_{y}\otimes H^{\prime}({\bm{k}}),\end{array}\right.

with
\sigma_{\mu}(\mu=x,y,z)
the Pauli matrices.
Then, we have the algebra in class AII:

\displaystyle{\rm AII}:\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}T^{\prime 2}=-1,\qquad T^{\prime}U_{g}^{\prime}({\bm{k}})=U_{g}^{\prime}(-{\bm{k}})T^{\prime},\\
\ U_{g}^{\prime}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}^{\prime}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}^{\prime}({\bm{k}}),\\
T^{\prime}H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})T^{\prime},\qquad U_{g}^{\prime}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}H(g{\bm{k}})\widetilde{U}_{g}^{\prime}({\bm{k}}).\end{array}\right.

An important point is that the obtained symmetry
U_{g}^{\prime}({\bm{k}})
behaves as
a PHS if
e^{i\theta_{g}}=-1
.
In a similar way, the factor systems for other AZ classes are
constructed by imposing additional chiral symmetries.

C.3
Superconductors with broken TRS

In a way similar to the previous section, we formulate the factor system for superconductors with broken TRS.
The only difference from the previous section is the absence of TRS.
There are no constraint relations among the TRS and space group symmetry, which means any 1-dimensional representations of the superconducting gap function in (
451
) are allowed.
We have the factor system for
n=2
(class D)

\displaystyle{\rm D}:\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}C^{2}=1,\qquad U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}}),\qquad CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C,\\
CH({\bm{k}})=-H(-{\bm{k}})C,\qquad U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(-{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}}).\end{array}\right.

Adding chiral symmetries with Eq. (
201
), we get the
factor systems for other AZ classes as summarized in
Table
19
.

and

are TRS and PHS, respectively,

represents the factor system for the point group.

C.4
Type III and IV magnetic space group symmetry

Let us consider the factor system for magnetic space (Shubnikov) group symmetry of type III and IV.
In such groups, there is no TRS itself.
An antiunitary symmetry appears as a combined symmetry with the TRS and a space group element.
Let us denote unitary and antiunitary symmetries by
U_{g}({\bm{k}})
and
A_{g}({\bm{k}})
, respectively, where
A_{g}({\bm{k}})
includes the complex conjugation.
Adding chiral symmetries, we have the factor systems for
n>0
as shown in Table
20
.

Table 20:
The factor systems for insulators with type III or IV magnetic space group symmetry.

\Gamma
is refereed as the chiral symmetry.

U_{g}({\bm{k}})
(
A_{g}({\bm{k}})
).

C.5
Superconductors with magnetic space group symmetry of type III and IV

Let us consider superconductors without TRS but preserving a combined symmetry between TRS and a space group symmetry, i.e. magnetic space group symmetry of type III and IV.
The derivation of the factor system is parallel to Sec.
C.2
.
Let
G=G_{0}+TgG_{0}
with
Tg
an antiunitary symmetry.
Using a
U(1)
phase rotation, one can assume the gap function is invariant under
Tg
,
U_{Tg}({\bm{k}})\Delta({\bm{k}})^{*}U_{Tg}(-{\bm{k}})^{T}=\Delta(-g{\bm{k}})
.
For unitary subgroup
G_{0}
, the gap function obeys a 1-dimensional irrep of
G_{0}
,
e^{i\theta_{h}}U_{h}\Delta({\bm{k}})U_{h}(-{\bm{k}})^{T}=\Delta(h{\bm{k}})
for
h\in G_{0}
.
We introduce
\hat{\tilde{h}}=\hat{h}e^{-\theta_{h}\hat{N}/2}
to restore the
G_{0}
symmetry.
The compatibility between
Tg
and
h\in G_{0}
leads to the condition
e^{i\theta_{h}}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
Then, the 1-dimensional irrep
e^{i\theta_{h}}(h\in G_{0})
can be extended to the irrep for
G
by putting
e^{i\theta_{Tg}}=1
.
With this 1-dimensional irrep for
G
, we introduce the symmetry operators acting on the BdG Hamiltonian as

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{g}({\bm{k}})=\begin{pmatrix}U_{g}({\bm{k}})&0\\
0&e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})^{*}\\
\end{pmatrix}\qquad g\in G_{0},

(473)

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{g}({\bm{k}})=\begin{pmatrix}U_{g}({\bm{k}})&0\\
0&e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})^{*}\\
\end{pmatrix}K,\qquad g\notin G_{0}.

(474)

We find that
CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C
and
CA_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C
.
The factor system for class D as well as other AZ classes is summarized in Table.
21
.

Table 21:
The factor systems for superconductors with type III or IV magnetic space group symmetry.
We denote the unitary (antiunitary) symmetry by
U_{g}({\bm{k}})
(
A_{g}({\bm{k}})
).

e^{i\theta_{g}}\in\{\pm 1\}
is a 1-dimensional irrep of
G
.

\begin{array}[]{l|l|l|l|l|l|l}{\rm AZ}&n&T,C&&&z_{g,h}&c(g)\\
\hline\cr{\rm D}&n=2&C^{2}=1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&CA_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr{\rm DIII}&n=3&T^{2}=-1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&TA_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})&A_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})A_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&CA_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})A_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}A_{gh}({\bm{k}})&\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm AII}&n=4&T^{2}=-1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&TA_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})A_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}A_{gh}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr{\rm CII}&n=5&T^{2}=-1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&TA_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&A_{g}(h{\bm{k}})A_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})&A_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=-H(g{\bm{k}})A_{g}({\bm{k}})\\

\hline\cr{\rm C}&n=6&C^{2}=-1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&CA_{g}({\bm{k}})=-e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr{\rm CI}&n=7&T^{2}=1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&TA_{g}({\bm{k}})=-e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})U_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})&A_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})A_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=-1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&CA_{g}({\bm{k}})=-e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})A_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}A_{gh}({\bm{k}})&\\
\cline{1-5}\cr\cline{7-7}\cr{\rm AI}&n=0&T^{2}=1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&TA_{g}({\bm{k}})=-e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&U_{g}(h{\bm{k}})A_{h}({\bm{k}})=z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}A_{gh}({\bm{k}})&U_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=H(g{\bm{k}})U_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
\cline{1-5}\cr{\rm BDI}&n=1&T^{2}=1&TU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&TA_{g}({\bm{k}})=-e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})T&A_{g}(h{\bm{k}})A_{h}({\bm{k}})=-z_{g,h}e^{-igh{\bm{k}}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{g,h}}U_{gh}({\bm{k}})&A_{g}({\bm{k}})H({\bm{k}})=-H(g{\bm{k}})A_{g}({\bm{k}})\\
&&C^{2}=1&CU_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}U_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&CA_{g}({\bm{k}})=e^{i\theta_{g}}A_{g}(-{\bm{k}})C&&\\
\hline\cr\end{array}
